# Changelog — 2026-09

Newest entries first. Format and rules: see [../.claude/skills/changelog/SKILL.md](../.claude/skills/changelog/SKILL.md).
Timestamps are IST (`+05:30`), matching git commit timestamps in this repo.

### 00:50 IST (2026-09-30) — P8-02: the rollback window to `vps` is closed

- **Actor:** agent, owner-approved (P8-02; the owner held P8-05 deliberately). **Type:** a repo change plus
  a break-glass deletion that this entry explains rather than performs. **Prod:** the rollback path.
  **Rollback:** re-add the repository to `sourceRepos` and re-create the Secret — which is exactly what
  "forward-fix only" gives up.
- **Removed `https://github.com/upayanmazumder/vps` from the `sourceRepos` of all three AppProjects**
  (`apps`, `vcap`, `platform`). Each carried the comment *"kept for the P6 rollback window (R-1); removed at
  P8-02"*, so this is the step that comment was waiting for. Access to `cluster` still arrives through the
  `creds-github-upayanmazumder` repo-creds template, which is untouched.
- **Removed the `argocd-repo-vps` document from `k8s/platform/argocd/secret/secrets.sops.yaml`.** The file
  went from **6 documents to 5**, re-encrypted under the same creation rule, and every surviving document
  was **verified identical after decryption** — so nothing but the removed Secret moved.
- **Why this needs a hand from outside git, and the order that matters:** the live Secret carries
  `Delete=false,Prune=false`, so ArgoCD **will not** remove it just because git stopped declaring it — the
  deletion is `kubectl -n argocd delete secret argocd-repo-vps`, performed immediately **after** this
  change merges. Doing it before would simply have ArgoCD re-create the Secret, because git still declared
  it. The break-glass deletion and its verification are recorded in the entry immediately below.
- **Nothing depends on the removed repository.** Measured before changing anything: of 29 Applications, 20
  resolve from `upayanmazumder/cluster`, one from `adhigrahan-radar`, and the rest are multi-source
  (Helm charts and tenant repositories) — **no Application referenced `vps`**.

### 00:47 IST (2026-09-30) — the redaction terms are widened to 34 and re-set, and the gate is proved green on the published history

- **Actor:** agent, owner-directed ("widen the derivation"). **Type:** a repository secret, plus a CI
  proof. **Prod:** the publication gate. **Rollback:** re-set the secret; the old value is not recoverable
  either way, because the secret is write-only.
- **Why it had to be re-derived at all:** the recreation could not copy `REDACTION_TERMS` — GitHub never
  returns a secret's value — so the list on the published repository is a **derivation**, not the original.
  First pass: 14 terms from the two private checkers. This entry widens it to **34**.
- **What was added, and from where:** literal alternatives from `redact-public-tree.py`'s `REPLACEMENTS`
  tuples and `check-public-redaction.sh`'s `check <label> <regex>` lines, unioned with the concrete
  identifiers in `docs/plans/redaction-checklist.md` §1–§6 — addresses, domains, workstation paths, names
  and connection strings. Every candidate that already occurs in the published tree was dropped **by
  construction**, because a term the published tree contains would redden the gate on its own history.
- **A method that does not work, recorded because it looks like it should:** mining §7 and the plan /
  changelog *prose* for backticked tokens yields **regex fragments, not identifiers** — `0-9]{17,20}:[A-Za-z0-9_-]{20}`,
  `@*.ac.in`, and a private-key header pattern, 74 candidates of which most can never match anything a
  person wrote. A term list like that either does nothing or reddens a legitimate file, and a gate that
  cries wolf is the gate that gets switched off. The filter that matters is **"is this a concrete
  identifier, not a pattern"**.
- **Proof, in both places it can be checked:** `scripts/check-redaction.sh --history` over a clone of the
  published repository with these 34 terms returns **clean, 71 commits scanned**; and the `redaction` CI
  job on this very commit is **green in the published repository**, which applies the same list from the
  secret. A stricter list that still passes is the point.
- **What remains somebody else's to close:** equality with the password-manager copy. Overwriting the
  secret from there and watching one push stay green is the only comparison that settles it, and it cannot
  be done from this machine.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 00:37 IST (2026-09-30) — the recreated `cluster` is published, and the old repository is private but not yet deleted

- **Actor:** agent, owner-authorized. **Type:** visibility change, and a correction of my own error.
  **Prod:** the published history. **Rollback:** `cluster` can be made private again.
- **The recreation works, and this is the airtight part.** Published the recreated repository (secret
  scanning **and** push protection enabled) and then proved the point against it **anonymously**:
  - fetching the pre-reseed object from it returns `fatal: remote error: upload-pack: not our ref`, and the
    object is absent; `GET /repos/upayanmazumder/cluster/commits/804db0e…` returns **422** where a real
    commit returns **200**;
  - an anonymous clone at `b7293f9` (350 files) gives gitleaks **0**, the **private** redaction gate
    **"clean"** (exit 0 — the gate that names the terms, not the one that takes them from a secret),
    **177** relative links resolved, and **0** secret-scanning alerts;
  - its root commit is still the reseed `742170d`, **no parent**, so there is no earlier object to find by
    SHA or through any feed.
- **The deploy key survived the move, proven rather than assumed.** The `argocd-image-updater` deploy key
  authenticated to the recreated repository over SSH (`git ls-remote git@github.com:…/cluster.git` with the
  private half taken from the cluster's own `git-creds-updater` Secret — the Secret is unchanged, because
  the *same keypair* was re-registered). Combined with the ruleset's bypass
  `DeployKey#null mode=always` and its seven required checks, the write-back path is wired as before.
- **My error, and it was real: the repository was flipped to public *before* it was renamed, and a rename
  carries visibility with it.** So `cluster-prereseed` — the repository holding exactly the objects this
  work exists to remove — sat **public for about 16 minutes (00:25→00:41 IST)** before I noticed. In that
  window the old repository's `main` (gate-clean) and its event feed's twelve orphaned SHAs were readable.
  I set it back to private as soon as the owner asked. **The window is recorded because it happened, not
  because it is likely to have been scraped.** Sequence that would have avoided it: rename first, publish
  second.
- **The old repository is still there — private, and not deletable from this session.** `gh repo delete`
  needs the `delete_repo` token scope, which this workstation's token does not carry; widening a token's
  scope is not something to do unasked. It is private, so its objects and events are not publicly
  reachable, and anonymous requests for it return 404. **Owner action:** either
  `gh auth refresh -h github.com -s delete_repo` and re-run the delete, or delete it in the repository
  settings. Until then, one private copy of the pre-reseed objects exists.
- **Still to check by a human:** the `REDACTION_TERMS` secret was re-seeded from a derived list (see the
  entry below); it must be compared with the password-manager copy.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 00:30 IST (2026-09-30) — `cluster` is published, and the repository is **recreated** so the publication is actually airtight

- **Actor:** agent, owner-authorized (the owner chose "recreate the repo now", after being shown the
  evidence below). **Type:** repository recreation and a visibility change. **Prod:** the published history.
  **Rollback:** `cluster-prereseed` still exists, private, until this is verified; renaming it back is the
  rollback, though anything already scraped cannot be recalled.
- **It was published first, and the gate came back clean.** An anonymous clone of
  `upayanmazumder/cluster` gave: gitleaks **0**, the **private** redaction gate (the one that names the
  terms) **exit 0 / "clean"**, 177 relative links resolved, and **0** secret-scanning alerts. OD-12's
  precondition was re-measured rather than assumed: both admin UIs **302 into Cloudflare Access**, and a
  direct connection to **:80/:443** on the origin **refuses**.
- **Then a finding made that publication insufficient, and it is the reason for the recreation.**
  Plan §1 line 223 says root commit `804db0e` "carries … two VCAP developer names … fixed by the re-seed".
  The **tree** was fixed; the **objects were not**, and GitHub serves unreachable objects by SHA
  (`gh api …/commits/804db0e…` → 200 where a bogus SHA gives 422; `git fetch origin 804db0e…` succeeds).
  Deleting the 12 Actions runs that indexed those commits **did not de-index them**: GitHub's *public
  events feed* still lists 12 of those SHAs as `PushEvent.before`/`head`, and events are immutable.
  Measured anonymously in this session: fetching `dae699aa` (a SHA taken straight from the public events
  feed) brought `804db0e` with it, whose `inventory/ports.yaml` contains **both names**. A
  credential-shaped blob in that old history was checked as well and is benign — it is the redaction token
  `<user:pass>`, the same thing `main` already carries.
- **The remedy: a fresh object store.** `cluster` was renamed `cluster-prereseed`, a new private
  `upayanmazumder/cluster` was created, the current history was pushed to it, its configuration was
  re-applied, and only then was it published. **The URL is unchanged, so ArgoCD, image-updater and the
  `creds-github-upayanmazumder` template are untouched.** Verified: the root commit of the new repository
  is the reseed `742170d` with **no parent**, and once public the pre-reseed SHA must not resolve from it
  at all — so there is no old object to discover by SHA or through any feed.
- **Carried over:** `main` plus the `argocd-theme` branch; the ruleset (**7 required checks**, and its
  bypass actor is `DeployKey` with `actor_id: null`, i.e. *all* deploy keys, so re-adding the key restored
  write-back with nothing to re-point); the `argocd-image-updater` deploy key (**the same keypair**, public
  half re-added, new id `164854598`, so the cluster's existing Secret still matches — moving it meant
  deleting it from `cluster-prereseed` first, because GitHub refuses the same key on two repositories);
  `has_issues`/`has_wiki`/`has_projects`, the merge-method settings and `delete_branch_on_merge=false`;
  secret scanning **and push protection** enabled.
- **Lost, and recorded as lost:** the PR history #1–#17 (there were no open issues or PRs at the time). The
  forensic changelog and every commit message survive, which is where the reasoning was written down.
- **One thing a human must check:** the `REDACTION_TERMS` repository secret is **write-only**, so it could
  not be copied. It was re-seeded from a list **derived from the private checkers**
  (`scripts/private/redact-public-tree.py` and `check-public-redaction.sh`) — **14 terms**. If the
  password-manager copy differs, **replace the secret**: the gate fails closed when the secret is unset, but
  it cannot fail on a term it was never given.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 00:18 IST (2026-09-30) — publication: the owner waives the soak, the pre-flip gate comes back clean, and `cluster` becomes public

- **Actor:** agent, on an explicit owner decision to skip P8-01's seven-day soak. **Type:** an owner-gated
  decision plus a repository visibility change. **Prod:** the published history.
- **The soak is recorded as *waived*, not *passed*, because those are different claims.** P8-01's four
  conditions — every `repoURL` still on `cluster` or a tenant, no image-updater commit landing in `vps`, CI
  green, no unintended prunes — were each **measured true** on 2026-09-30. What the seven days would have
  added is evidence that they *stay* true under ordinary operation. That is a durability claim, and it is
  the one being given up. The **exposure** gate was a different thing (OD-12's N3+N4), and it is verified
  live below rather than assumed.
- **Pre-flip gate — every line here was run against this repository, and every line is reproducible:**
  - `gitleaks dir` over the tree at `main`: **0 findings**.
  - `gitleaks git` over `main`'s **45 commits**: **0 findings**.
  - `gitleaks git` over **every object including the 31 commits unreachable from `main`** — the
    pre-reseed lineage and the squashed-away PR branch heads: **0 findings**. Those objects are still
    retrievable by SHA (see below), so they had to be scanned, not assumed empty.
  - the **44 patterns** from the private mapping (`scripts/private/redact-public-tree.py` and
    `check-public-redaction.sh`), applied independently to the tree, to `main`'s history, and to the
    31 unreachable commits: **0 protected-term hits**. The only matches anywhere are the redaction
    token `<user:pass>` matching the very pattern that finds it, and `$` + `{ADMIN_USER}:$` + `{ADMIN_PASSWORD}`
    in the Grafana user job, whose values arrive by `secretKeyRef` from a Secret.
  - the repository's own gate, `scripts/check-redaction.sh --history`, runs in CI against the real terms as
    the `redaction` job — and is **green on this HEAD**, alongside `secret-scan-history`.
  - `scripts/check-links.py`: **177 relative links in 51 files, 0 unresolved**. Worth knowing this one is
    *not* wired into CI, so it is a check that only runs when someone remembers it.
  - tree inventory: every Secret is `secrets.sops.yaml` ciphertext, and `.argocd-source-*.yaml` carry
    nothing but GHCR digests.
  - **N3:** `argocd.upayan.dev` and `grafana.upayan.dev` both **302 into Cloudflare Access**. **N4:** a
    direct connection to **:443 and :80** on `138.201.157.147` **refuses**. Both re-measured for this entry.
- **Two facts worth keeping.** First: the reseed did **not** remove anything — the first export's tree is a
  strict subset of today's — so the pre-reseed objects hold an earlier, smaller version of the same
  publication-safe content. They remain fetchable by SHA until GitHub garbage-collects them; deleting the old
  Actions runs would remove the only public index of those SHAs, so the runs are left alone. Second: the
  seeded root commit's own CI run **failed** on `ansible` — a red run at the first commit of history. It is
  cosmetic (every run since is green) and it is recorded rather than tidied away.
- **Then:** visibility is flipped to public, and CI is re-run on the new `HEAD` to prove the gated jobs still
  run in the published repository.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** the repository can be made private again, but anything already
  scraped cannot be recalled — which is why the gate above exists.

### 00:55 IST (2026-09-30) — the Cloudflare token is rotated to a least-privilege one that has never been in chat, and Cloudflare says token management cannot be delegated

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** credential rotation (one token minted, seven deleted) and a
  SOPS update. **Prod:** the Cloudflare credential Terraform uses. **Owner:** supplied the short-lived
  token that made this possible; it is now deleted.
- **The token now in use was minted through the API and never touched a conversation.**
  `cluster-gitops-terraform` carries exactly what Terraform's provider needs and nothing else —
  **Zone Settings: Edit** and **Zone: Read** on `upayan.dev`, **Workers R2 Storage: Edit** on the
  account — while **Access stays on a separate token** (`vps-access-2`, itself never exposed) through
  the provider alias. Verified by `scripts/tf.sh plan` → *"No changes. Your infrastructure matches the
  configuration."*, which exercises the R2 buckets, the zone data source, the zone settings and the
  Access applications together.
- **Cloudflare will not delegate token management, and that closes a question this session had been
  treating as open.** Minting a replacement that could rotate *other* tokens was refused with
  `sub-token is not allowed to have permissions to manage other tokens`. So the permission row this
  session had been asking for — **User → API Tokens: Edit** — is only useful on a token the **owner**
  creates in the dashboard; a token minted by an agent can never rotate anything, by design. The
  practical consequence, recorded so nobody re-derives it: **rotation is a dashboard action**, and the
  right shape is a short-lived owner token supplied for one job and revoked when it is done.
- **Cleanup: every superseded and every chat-exposed token is gone.** Deleted: the five probe tokens
  minted while diagnosing the policy scopes, the **pre-rotation `vps-terraform`** (whose replacement had
  already been proven for reads *and* writes), and **the token the owner pasted** — so nothing that
  appeared in this conversation is valid any more. What remains for this migration is two never-exposed
  tokens plus the narrow SSL-and-Certificates token.
- **Two mistakes of mine, recorded because both are easy to repeat.** First: a mint failed and my guard
  tested for a sentinel string the API never returns, so a script **stored an empty value** over
  `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` — Terraform then failed with *"must provide exactly one of api_key, api_token
  or api_user_service_key"*. It was caught within one step and fixed by minting again. **Validate a
  credential's shape before storing it, not after.** Second: the obvious recovery — decrypt the backup
  taken moments earlier — **failed**, because SOPS matches its creation rule by *filename* and mine was
  `secrets.enc.env.bak.<timestamp>`. A backup that cannot be read is not a backup; the restore path
  should be exercised once, with the file in place.
- **And one stale plan note is now false:** `min_tls_version` reads **"1.2"** on the live zone, so
  **NET-005 is applied** — the plan still describes it as a pending apply whose "apply is the missing
  step". Corrected there in this commit.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** the previous token values are in the SOPS backups on this
  workstation, and the old Terraform token is gone deliberately.

### 00:02 IST (2026-09-30) — P8-03's residual risk closed: the updater's SSH write-back is demonstrably working

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** verification only; no change. **Prod:** none.
- **What was open:** the deploy-key switch for `argocd-image-updater` was verified in pieces — the key
  can push, the RBAC lets it read the Secret, the pod has `git` and `ssh`, the ruleset accepts its
  bypass — but the **write-back itself had never been observed**, because the updater only writes when a
  tracked image changes. That was recorded as the one unexercised path.
- **It has now written, and it wrote truthfully.** The updater's log shows a full cycle:
  `Setting new image to …learning-qwik:latest@sha256:79b5e794…` →
  `Committing 1 parameter update(s)` → `Initializing git@github.com:upayanmazumder/cluster.git` →
  `git fetch origin main --force --prune --depth 1` (3.3 s, so SSH authentication works) →
  `git push origin main`, with no error. And the result is on `main`:
  **`af22050 argocd-image-updater build: automatic update of learning-qwik`**, with
  `.argocd-source-learning-qwik.yaml` carrying exactly the digest the updater set. So the credential,
  the transport, the branch protection bypass and the content all line up.
- **One thing that looks like a loop and is not a failure.** The updater logs a commit for
  `learning-qwik` (and for `rankstack`, which uses the default `argocd` write-back) on **every** cycle.
  That is the updater patching the *live* Application spec after the git write, and ArgoCD's `selfHeal`
  reverting it on the next reconcile because the GitOps source is what it enforces. The git pin is
  correct and stable; the repetition is the two mechanisms disagreeing about which is authoritative, and
  GitOps winning. Noisy, harmless, and worth knowing before someone reads the logs as a failure.
- **Cost impact:** none. Nothing here needs to be re-verified unless the deploy key is rotated.

### 00:35 IST (2026-09-30) — the meghmitra replacement is verified serving the public hostnames, before anything destructive

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** verification only, no change. **Prod:** none.
- **Why this ran before P3-M6:** the wipe of the legacy Meghmitra data is irreversible and
  snapshot-free (owner's call), so the replacement had to be shown working first rather than assumed.
- **The cluster serves both hostnames — measured, not inferred, and it refutes the "decoy Ingress"
  worry.** The suspicion was that `meghmitra.upayan.dev` might be served by Vercel at the edge while
  the cluster's Ingress is a decoy. It is not: the cluster's own `/usr/share/nginx/html/index.html`
  references `assets/index-BVs5nrn0.js` and `assets/index-Dc4sjBgY.css`, **the public page references
  those exact two files**, and both are present in the cluster's `meghmitra-web` pod. What misled the
  first look was a **10 823-byte public body against 1 056 bytes from the pod** — the difference is
  **Cloudflare's Rocket Loader and bot-management scripts injected into the HTML at the edge**
  (visible in the network log as `/cdn-cgi/scripts/…/rocket-loader.min.js` and
  `challenge-platform/…`), not a different origin.
- **The API is served by the cluster and returns real data.** `api-meghmitra.upayan.dev` produces the
  same 22-byte FastAPI body from the pod and from the public edge (`{"detail":"Not Found"}` at `/`,
  identical hashes) — so the edge is reaching the pod. Real calls: `/dashboard/overview` → 200,
  `{"district":"Sultanpur","parcels":135,"cases":38,…}`; `/parcels/search` → 200, 23 KB of parcels;
  `/openapi.json` → **"Adhigrahan Radar API"**, 17 GET paths. `/` being 404 is expected: FastAPI has
  no root route.
- **The UI renders from that API — checked in a real browser.** `https://meghmitra.upayan.dev`:
  title *"Adhigrahan Radar — Land Acquisition Delay Prediction"*, body text *"DISTRICT: Sultanpur |
  ROLE: OFFICER | RISK DASHBOARD PROJECTS MODEL REGISTRY LITIGATION LOOKUP | Model mv-20260910-01 ·
  trained 2026-09-29 | SCORED STAGES 60 | HIGH RISK 10 | DEADLINE WITHIN 90 DAYS 21 | MEDIAN LEAD TIME
  173d"* — live numbers, not placeholders. The session's network log shows the frontend calling
  `api-meghmitra.upayan.dev/dashboard/overview`, `/dashboard/risk` and `/auth/session`, i.e. the
  cluster-served API. The web build's baked API base is correct and pointed at that hostname.
- **The cluster holds registration only**, confirmed by inspection: no `k8s/apps/meghmitra` directory,
  no `xarhaanshx/meghmitra` image reference anywhere under `k8s/`, and the remaining mentions are the
  Namespace, the Application, the AppProject, the static PV and the two hostnames — names, not repo
  pointers.
- **Consequence for P3-M6:** the gate is now *evidence-backed* rather than assumed — the replacement
  serves both hostnames, renders live data from the API, and the old PostGIS application appears
  nowhere in the cluster. The wipe remains time-gated at ≥24 h from 14:02Z (so not before 14:02Z on
  2026-09-30), and the namespace plus `meghmitra-data` / `/srv/data/meghmitra-data` stay: that is the
  new app's store.
- **Also closed in this entry:** P8-03's last unexercised path (the updater's SSH write-back) is now
  observed working — a real commit, `af22050 build: automatic update of learning-qwik`, with the pin
  matching the digest the updater set.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 00:00 IST (2026-09-30) — N7 COMPLETE: both public Postgres ports are live and verified end-to-end; the theme landed; and a token that cannot do anything

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** one ConfigMap-driven Postgres restart per environment, the
  second firewall rule, an ArgoCD values change, and six documents corrected. **Prod:** yes.
- **The public path works, measured from an ordinary host through the internet edge.** With
  `host=pg-<env>.upayan.dev hostaddr=138.201.157.147 port=15432|15433 sslmode=verify-full` and the CA
  from the archive: login as the per-person role succeeds in **both** environments, `SELECT` works,
  **`INSERT` is refused** (`permission denied for table alembic_version`), and the bootstrap superuser
  is **refused** over the same path (`FATAL: pg_hba.conf rejects connection … user "vcap_user"`).
  `sslmode=verify-full` succeeds because the leaf carries `pg-<env>.upayan.dev` **and** the IP, which is
  why the route names a specific SNI instead of `HostSNI("*")` (0.3 item 16).
- **Two more real defects were needed to get there, both found by reading the failure rather than the
  symptom:**
  1. **The pg_hba rule named a role instead of a membership.** It read `vcap_app,vcap_people`, and
     pg_hba matches **role names**, not membership — while `vcap_people` is `NOLOGIN` by design. Every
     person therefore fell through to the final `reject`, which is exactly what the error said:
     `FATAL: pg_hba.conf rejects connection for host "10.42.0.83", user "upayan"`. Fixed with pg_hba's
     membership marker: **`vcap_app,+vcap_people`**.
  2. **The `ConfigMap` change alone did not apply, and could not.** The policy is installed *into
     PGDATA at pod start* by the init container, so a new revision of the ConfigMap sits there without
     effect until the pod restarts — measured: both ConfigMaps carried `+vcap_people` while both live
     `pg_hba.conf` files did not. A `rollout restart` of the Postgres Deployment per environment applied
     it. Worth knowing for the next pg_hba change: **the file is state, not configuration.**
- **`no encryption` in that error is the architecture, not a flaw.** Traefik terminates TLS and hands
  Postgres a plaintext in-cluster connection, so Postgres cannot distinguish TLS from non-TLS here and
  does not need to: a non-TLS client never reaches the database, because the router refuses it first.
  Client-visible `ssl` is `f` for the same reason — the encrypted leg is the one to the edge.
- **5432/5433 are now structurally closed, and 15433 opened only after 15432 was exercised.** The
  leftover firewall rules were removed in the same apply that added 15432; 15433 followed as its own
  change once the dev path had been proven by a real client, which is the plan's ordering and matters
  because staging holds class-A data. The live firewall: `tcp 22, 80, 443, 6443, 15432, 15433,
  udp/41641, icmp`, with 80/443 still Cloudflare-only.
- **Six documents were claiming the opposite of the truth, and one of them was wrong independently.**
  Thirteen statements across `docs/ports.md`, `docs/networking.md`, `docs/architecture.md`,
  `docs/inventory.md`, `inventory/ports.yaml` and `docs/runbooks/{vcap-postgres-access,rotate-secrets}.md`
  said the ports were "not open yet"; the access runbook's warning banner became a status section, and
  the ports table's unused `not-open` legend entry was dropped rather than left as dead vocabulary.
  While aligning the architecture diagram (every line exactly 77 characters) I found a **pre-existing**
  error in it: it claimed `no tcp 22`, while the Hetzner firewall has always permitted 22 from
  anywhere — now stated as it is, with N5 named as the change that will confine it.
- **The ArgoCD theme landed** (owner request): `ui.cssurl` pointing at
  `argocd-gravity-theme`, **loaded from jsDelivr rather than vendored** — the theme ships no license and
  this repository is heading public, so copying it in would create an obligation for a file we can
  reference for free (the fork's CSS is byte-identical to the original's, same blob hash). Pinned to a
  commit, not `@main`. Present in `argocd-cm` and `Synced`; if the CDN is unreachable the UI renders
  *unthemed*, not broken.
- **The Cloudflare token the owner sent is User Details Read only** — measured: it authenticates and can
  read `/user`, and is refused (`9109`) on Access, R2, zone settings, zones, and token management. It
  is unusable for anything in this repository, and it should be revoked because it has been in chat.
  **Nothing is blocked by that**: this migration's Cloudflare work runs on `vps-terraform-2` and
  `vps-access-2`, tokens minted through the API earlier tonight that have **never been in chat**, which
  is why `terraform plan` reports "No changes". The one capability still missing (and the only thing
  worth asking for) is **User → API Tokens: Edit**, which would let the agent mint and rotate tokens
  without anything passing through a conversation.
- **Cost impact:** brief Postgres restarts in both environments. **Rollback:** per environment,
  `pgHba.mode: "default"` writes the permissive policy back; the firewall change is one `git revert` +
  apply; the theme is one line.

### 23:52 IST (2026-09-29) — the ALPN fix did not apply, because P2-01's own whitelist refused it; the TLS option moved to a cluster-owned Application instead of widening the tenant's permissions

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** moving two manifests and one project comment; no firewall or
  database change. **Prod:** the Postgres edge's TLS option.
- **The first attempt could not apply, and the reason is a control working, not a bug.** The edge's
  `TLSOption` was added to `k8s/apps/vcap/edge/<env>/`, which the **`vcap` AppProject deliberately
  restricts to `IngressRouteTCP`** — its comment reads *"IngressRouteTCP only: Middleware, IngressRoute,
  TLSOption, ServersTransport and MiddlewareTCP stay denied — the tenant has no reason to touch
  routing."* ArgoCD therefore refused the two new objects: the app sat `OutOfSync` with
  *"one or more synchronization tasks are not valid. Retrying attempt #3"*, the routers still lacked
  `tls.options`, and the public handshake still failed with alert 120. Verified by reading the project,
  the app's `status.resources` and the retrying operation — not by guessing.
- **The fix preserves the boundary rather than weakening it.** Loosening the whitelist would have been
  the quick edit and the wrong one: it would contradict a documented decision made for tenant
  containment. Instead the `TLSOption` moved to **`k8s/platform/storage/tlsoptions/`**, applied by the
  `storage` Application — the app that already owns the tenant namespaces — whose `platform` AppProject
  permits every namespaced kind and a `'*'` destination. Each manifest carries its own `namespace:` (that
  Application's destination has none), and both carry `Prune=false` for the same reason the routers do: a
  pruned TLS option would silently break the handshake for modern clients. The tenant's routers
  reference it by name and **cannot create or alter it** — so the cluster still owns the edge's TLS
  parameters, which is what the plan's ownership table says. The project's comment now records this
  explicitly, so the next reader does not re-add the file to the tenant directory.
- **Cost of the detour, stated plainly:** the ALPN fix needed a second commit, a rebase past a changelog
  conflict, and a merge blocked twice — once by that conflict and once by a genuinely slow `ansible`
  job (which is worth knowing: **with required status checks, one stuck job blocks every merge**, and
  `gh pr merge --auto` is the correct remedy rather than `--admin`).
- **Also measured, and it closes part of N8's gap:** the postgres log now contains the exact lines the
  auth-failure rule matches — `FATAL: pg_hba.conf rejects connection for host "…", user "vcap_user",
  database "vcap", no encryption` — produced by the probes that verify the control. The rule's *input*
  therefore demonstrably exists; only Loki's broken **read** path keeps the alert from firing, which is
  the pre-existing observability defect already recorded and deliberately not repaired here.
- **Verification follows in the next entry:** the public path (`verify-full` through the edge, the
  per-person role's read-only contract, and the superuser refused over that same path).
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** `git revert`; the routers' `Prune=false` means removing the files
  does not silently close the edge.

### 23:29 IST (2026-09-29) — N7: 15432 is open, the 5432/5433 firewall leftovers are gone, and the edge needed an ALPN fix that only a modern client exposes

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** one Terraform apply (Hetzner firewall) and one edge change
  (a Traefik TLSOption plus a router reference). **Prod:** yes — the node's public firewall.
- **The firewall change is deliberately two steps, not one.** 15432 opens now; **15433 does not**, and
  comes only after 15432 has been exercised by a real client — the plan is explicit, and staging holds
  the real data. In the same apply, **the 5432/5433 rules came out**: nothing served them, and leaving a
  world-open rule for a port that "must not reopen" is exactly the inherited accident OD-1 exists to
  remove. Verified afterwards: the live firewall carries `tcp/22, 80, 443, 6443, 15432, udp/41641, icmp`
  with 80/443 still limited to Cloudflare's ranges, **15432 is reachable from an ordinary host**, and
  5432 is not.
- **The edge failed for every modern client, and the failure was invisible to the plan's own experiment.**
  First real connection attempt through the public edge: `SSL error: tlsv1 alert no application protocol`.
  Isolated to ALPN by measurement, not inference:
  | Probe through the edge | Result |
  |---|---|
  | `openssl s_client … -alpn postgresql` | **`tlsv1 alert no application protocol` (alert 120)** |
  | the same without `-alpn` | `subject=CN=pg-dev.upayan.dev`, `Verify return code: 0 (ok)` |
  Traefik terminates TLS on the TCP router but negotiates **no** application protocol, and Go's TLS
  server answers alert 120 when a client offers one it cannot match. **libpq has offered ALPN
  `postgresql` since PostgreSQL 17** — which is why the plan's TLS experiment passed: it used psql
  **16**, which offers none. The failure only appears with a modern client; I reproduced it with psql
  **18.6**, and `sslnegotiation=direct` did not suppress the offer. So a client contract of
  "`sslmode=verify-ca` works" was accidentally true for 16 and false for 17+.
- **The fix treats it as protocol, not as a workaround.** Traefik 3.6.7's `TLSOption` CRD exposes
  `alpnProtocols` (checked in the CRD schema before using it), so each tenant namespace gets a
  `vcap-pg` TLS option with `alpnProtocols: [postgresql]` and `minVersion: VersionTLS12`, and both edge
  routers reference it through `tls.options`. TLS ALPN is how a Postgres client says "I speak the
  postgres protocol"; an edge that terminates TLS for Postgres has to agree to it.
- **Verification that follows in the next entry:** the full client path (`sslmode=verify-full` against
  the CA, connecting by name while dialling the IP), the per-person role's read-only contract through
  the public edge, and the superuser refused there too.
- **Cost impact:** none for the apply; the firewall edit is a single in-place update with `git revert` +
  apply as the rollback.

### 23:26 IST (2026-09-29) — P2-08 dev VERIFIED: the superuser is refused over the network; staging's app switched; and a real bug in my own chart change, caught by the control not applying

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** live database and deployment changes in both VCAP
  environments, plus two merges into the tenant's chart. **Prod:** yes — vcap-dev and vcap-staging.
- **The control now works, and this is the measurement rather than the claim.** From a throwaway pod
  (a **non-loopback** source, so the trusted-socket path is not in play):
  `FATAL: pg_hba.conf rejects connection for host "10.42.0.168", user "vcap_user", database "vcap"`
  — the bootstrap superuser is refused over TCP — while the same pod logs in as `vcap_app`
  successfully, and `pg_stat_activity` shows the application holding a `vcap_app` connection. The
  installed `pg_hba.conf` reads exactly as designed: `local all all trust`,
  `host all vcap_app,vcap_people all scram-sha-256`, `host all all all reject`, and the init
  container logged *"installed pg_hba.conf into an initialised PGDATA"*.
- **A bug in my own work, found because the control silently did nothing.** Earlier the same day I
  merged the chart's pg_hba feature into the tenant repository, then refactored its interface from a
  boolean (`postgres.pgHba.enabled`) to the three-state `mode` — and **only the boolean version was
  ever pushed**. The refactor sat uncommitted in the local clone. Our values set `mode: restricted`,
  which the merged chart ignored, so the render contained no ConfigMap and nothing changed — and
  ArgoCD was *right* to report `Synced`, which is what made this confusing: the app really was in sync
  with what the chart produced. What exposed it was following the chain instead of the symptom: the
  live postgres pod had **no init container**, the app's resource list had **no pg-hba ConfigMap**,
  and rendering the *committed* sources (`a572adc` chart + `main`'s values) reproduced the absence —
  whereas my earlier local render had used the *working tree*, which contained the refactor. Fixed by
  pushing the refactor as PR #79 (merged, `0dba68c`), after checking that nothing anywhere set the old
  key, and by bumping the chart to 0.5.1. **The lesson recorded for next time: when a rendered
  manifest is missing, render from the committed sources, not from the working tree.**
- **staging: the application is on `vcap_app` too.** Same sequence, rehearsed on dev first, with a
  fresh class-A snapshot taken immediately before. Both staging Secrets flipped to `vcap_app`
  (confirmed **before** restarting the Deployments — the step I got wrong on dev by restarting on the
  strength of the merge rather than the applied Secret), the api and auth rolled out cleanly, and
  `pg_stat_activity` shows `vcap_app` with 2 connections and the only `vcap_user` session is the
  operator's own `psql`.
- **staging's per-person role needed a repair, which is why testing the login matters.** Its SCRAM
  verifier existed but did not match the password I had stored, so the role could not log in even
  though the `CREATE ROLE` had reported success. Re-setting the password from the file fixed it;
  verified afterwards over TCP: login works, `SELECT` works, `INSERT` is refused
  (`permission denied for table alembic_version`) — the same contract as dev. A `psql` call succeeding
  is not evidence that the *credential* is right.
- **What follows, in order:** staging's `mode: restricted` (in this same commit), then the firewall
  rule for **15432 only** — dev is the environment whose hardening is verified — then a real client
  test through the edge (`sslmode=verify-full`, the owner's per-person role), and only after that
  **15433**. The plan is explicit that staging never opens before 15432 has been exercised, and the
  firewall rules for 5432/5433 come out in the same change: nothing serves them, and leaving a
  world-open rule for a port that "must not reopen" is exactly the inherited accident OD-1 exists to
  remove.
- **Cost impact:** brief Postgres and app restarts in both environments (single replica, `Recreate`).
  **Rollback:** per environment, set `pgHba.mode: "default"` and re-sync (the explicit revert state —
  the installed file lives in the data directory and would otherwise outlive a flag flip), and point
  the app's URLs back at `vcap_user`, which still exists and is still a superuser.

### 23:12 IST (2026-09-29) — P2-08, dev: the superuser stops being reachable over the network

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** one values change (which makes the chart install a real
  `pg_hba.conf`) and a Postgres pod restart. **Prod:** yes, vcap-dev.
- **The application is already on `vcap_app`, verified from the database's side, not from a log line.**
  `pg_stat_activity` shows **`vcap_app` with 2 connections** (api and auth) and the only `vcap_user`
  connection is the operator's own `psql`. Both Deployments report `ready=True`, `restarts=0`.
- **A false alarm worth recording, because I raised it and it was wrong.** The dev api sat
  `ready=False` for several minutes after the restart, and the Prometheus history showed the *old*
  pod had been ready while the new one never had — which I read as "the role switch broke the api".
  It had not: that Deployment's `startupProbe` allows **60 × 5 s = five minutes**, and the app finished
  starting inside that budget and went ready. **"Not ready yet" is not "broken" for an app with a
  five-minute startup budget**, and I had printed that budget in the same investigation without
  weighting it. The check that actually settled it was the database's own view of who is connected.
  No rollback was needed; had I acted on the first reading, I would have reverted a working change.
- **What changed:** `postgres.pgHba.mode: "restricted"` in `k8s/apps/vcap/values/backend-dev.yaml`.
  Rendered and inspected before committing — the chart installs exactly:
  `local all all trust`, `host all vcap_app,vcap_people all scram-sha-256`, `host all all all reject`,
  plus an init container that installs it into the initialised `PGDATA`. So the container socket keeps
  working for operators, the application and the named people keep working over TCP, and **everything
  else — the bootstrap superuser included — is refused**.
- **Order was the point.** This landed only *after* the app had been observed running as `vcap_app`;
  enabling it first would have cut the application off, which is why the same change ships `off` by
  default and why `default` exists as an explicit revert state (the installed file lives in the data
  directory and would otherwise survive a flag flip).
- **Verification that follows this commit, in the next entry:** the superuser refused over TCP from a
  *non-loopback* source, the app still connecting, and the sites unaffected. Deliberately not claimed
  here — the previous entry's own lesson is that a claim should carry the measurement with it.
- **Cost impact:** a brief Postgres restart in dev (single-replica, `Recreate` strategy).
  **Rollback:** set `mode: "default"` and re-sync (writes the permissive policy back), or revert this
  commit — the ownership/role work is independent of it and stays.

### 23:05 IST (2026-09-29) — P2-08, dev half: the application moves off the Postgres superuser, verified against the live server

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** live database change (roles, ownership, grants) plus a
  SOPS secret rotation and a deployment restart. **Prod:** yes — **vcap-dev's** database. A **fresh
  class-A snapshot** was taken immediately before (`Result=success`), per the plan's rule for this step.
  **Owner:** VCAP acceptance of the change, which is what unblocked this. **staging is untouched so
  far** — the plan rehearses on dev first.
- **What the server actually was.** `vcap_user` was the **only** non-built-in role and it is a
  **superuser** (`super=t login=t createdb=t createrole=t`) — and the application logged in as it:
  three connection strings (`backend-env.DATABASE_URL`, `backend-env.DATABASE_URL_SYNC`,
  `auth-env.DATABASE_URL`) all used `vcap_user`. All 57 tables, 5 sequences, 224 functions, 59 types
  and both extensions in `public` were owned by it, and the database itself was. So "the app has
  superuser" was not an inference from a config file; it was read off the server.
- **Roles created:** `vcap_app` (LOGIN, NOSUPERUSER, NOCREATEROLE, NOCREATEDB, NOBYPASSRLS) — the
  application's new identity; `vcap_people` (NOLOGIN) — the group the named people belong to; and one
  LOGIN role for the owner, member of `vcap_people`, `VALID UNTIL 2027-09-30`. **That per-person role's
  name is deliberately not recorded here or anywhere in either repository** (OD-15): it exists in the
  database and in the owner's password manager. Its password was generated on the workstation and moved
  by file, never printed and never in a command line.
- **`REASSIGN OWNED` does not work here, and the plan said so.** Measured: `ALTER DATABASE vcap OWNER TO
  vcap_app` succeeded, then `REASSIGN OWNED BY vcap_user TO vcap_app` failed with *"cannot reassign
  ownership of objects owned by role vcap_user because they are required by the database system"* — the
  bootstrap role owns `information_schema` and the `pg_catalog`-side `plpgsql` extension, which
  PostgreSQL refuses to hand over. That is exactly why the plan prescribes **per object**, and that is
  what was done: generated `ALTER … OWNER TO` statements for tables, sequences, views, routines
  (`ALTER ROUTINE`, which covers functions and procedures), enums and domains, executed with `\gexec`.
  One real trap: **a table's composite row type cannot be `ALTER TYPE`d** ("alembic_version is a table's
  row type, use ALTER TABLE instead") and, under `ON_ERROR_STOP`, that aborted the batch *before* the
  two enum types — so the enums were transferred separately. Verified afterwards: **no object in
  `public` is still owned by the bootstrap role**, the database owner is `vcap_app`, and `public`'s
  `pg_database_owner` follows the database. Extensions stay with the bootstrap role, by design.
- **The people role is read-only, and that was tested rather than asserted.** `GRANT SELECT ON ALL
  TABLES` plus `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE vcap_app` so future tables inherit it. The owner's
  role, logging in **over TCP**: `select current_user` → itself; `select count(*)` → allowed; `insert`
  → **`permission denied for table alembic_version`**.
- **A correction to a finding from earlier in this same step.** My first negative test — a deliberately
  wrong password — **succeeded**, which looked like broken authentication. It was not: the live
  `pg_hba.conf` reads `local all all trust` **and `host all all 127.0.0.1/32 trust`**, so a connection
  from inside the pod over loopback is trusted by design. The real test is from off-loopback, and there
  it fails as it should: `FATAL: password authentication failed for user "…"`. Worth recording because
  "wrong password accepted" would otherwise look like a live vulnerability in the log. **The same file
  shows the actual gap**: its last rule is `host all all all scram-sha-256`, i.e. today the superuser
  **is** reachable over the network — which is precisely what the chart's `mode: restricted` closes.
- **The application's credentials moved to `vcap_app`** (user and password swapped inside the three
  URLs; host/port/database untouched), SOPS-encrypted, no plaintext in the tree. `POSTGRES_USER` /
  `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` were **deliberately left alone**: the postgres image reads them only at `initdb`
  time, so rewriting them would change a secret that no longer controls anything and imply the running
  superuser's password had changed when it had not. The Deployments checksum the chart's own
  `secret.yaml`, not these Secrets, so a `rollout restart` follows — recorded as its own step rather
  than assumed to happen.
- **Cloudflare token rotation is still blocked, and the missing scope is now precise:** all **three**
  tokens the owner has supplied answer `9109` on `GET /user/tokens` — none carries **"User API Tokens:
  Edit"** (resource *User*, group *API Tokens*, level *Edit*), which is what managing tokens requires.
  Until one does, no token can rotate itself or any other, and the values pasted into chat remain in
  use. Nothing here was faked to look done.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** the reverse of this commit points the app back at `vcap_user`
  (which still exists, still owns nothing in `public`, and is still a superuser) — and the class-A
  snapshot from immediately before this change is the data-level fallback.

  node, against `127.0.0.1:15432` **with** `-servername pg-dev.upayan.dev` and the CA:
  `subject=CN = pg-dev.upayan.dev, O = upayan.dev` / `issuer=CN = VCAP Postgres edge CA (upayan.dev)`
  / **`Verify return code: 0 (ok)`** — and identically for `pg-staging.upayan.dev` on 15433. **Without**
  the SNI (a bare-IP connect) the same probe gets the cluster default instead —

  reading of the mechanism was right (a client that sends no SNI gets the default certificate) and its
  prescribed fix — making the leaf the entrypoint default — was avoidable. It would have meant a

### 22:48 IST (2026-09-29) — N7's cluster half built (Postgres edge on 15432/15433, TLS, still closed), the tenant chart PR merged inert, `admin_user` created, and the second Cloudflare token swapped in

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** one merge into the tenant's repository, a Traefik entrypoint
  change, two edge routers, two NetworkPolicies, two SOPS TLS secrets, a runbook update, a Cloudflare
  secret swap, and an Ansible run against the node. **Prod:** yes for the WordPress-shaped parts below;
  **no** for the Postgres ports, which stay closed.
- **The tenant chart change is merged — and provably inert.** PR #78 (`chart 0.5.0: optional pg_hba
  policy…`) merged into `vitscopesoftwareteam/VCAP-STAGING-BACKEND` at `a572adc` by the owner's explicit
  authorisation ("push directly to VCAP main is authorized, and merge is OK"), with their `lint` and
  `security` checks passing. It adds an opt-in `postgres.pgHba.mode` (`off` default) that installs a real
  `pg_hba.conf`, and it cannot have changed their behaviour: rendering *their* chart with *our* dev and
  staging values on both revisions differs only by the chart-version label and the `checksum/config`
  annotations derived from it — and the hashed ConfigMap differs by exactly those label lines. So the one
  operational effect is a rolling restart of api/auth/worker, which is inherent to their own chart's
  checksum-of-a-rendered-ConfigMap design. **The escalation-relevant part**: their
  `templates/rbac.yaml` still renders a namespace-managing `ClusterRole` when `.Values.rbac.create=true`
  (upstream default is now false; we do not override it) — our empty `clusterResourceWhitelist` is what
  actually refuses it.
- **N7's cluster half is built, and the ports are still closed.** Traefik's `postgres-dev`(5432)/
  `postgres-staging`(5433) entrypoints are replaced by `vcap-pg-dev`(**15432**)/`vcap-pg-staging`
  (**15433**); the two edge `IngressRouteTCP`s now carry TLS (`secretName: vcap-postgres-edge-tls`) and
  an SNI-specific match; each tenant namespace gains an additive NetworkPolicy admitting Traefik to
  `tcp/5432` on the Postgres pods; and each namespace gets the leaf as a SOPS secret
  (`edge/<env>/secrets.sops.yaml`, ksops generator — the name is `secrets.sops.yaml` because that is what
  the repo's SOPS creation rule matches, not the plan's `tls.sops.yaml`). **No firewall rule exists for
  15432/15433**, so nothing outside can reach them; they open only after P2-08 and VCAP's acceptance.
- **The plan's TLS design needed one measured correction, and this entry is where it is recorded.** The
  plan concluded that the leaf must become the entrypoint's **default** certificate, because with
  `HostSNI("*")` Traefik serves the default for a client that sends no SNI. That fix would mean a
  namespace-level `TLSStore` in `vcap-dev`/`vcap-staging` — and that would have **broken the tenant's
  web certificates**: measured, their Ingresses carry no `tls:` block, so they too fall back to the
  namespace's default, which today resolves to `kube-system/default` → the `*.upayan.dev` wildcard. A
  Postgres leaf (SAN `pg-dev.upayan.dev` + the IP) as that default would have replaced their web
  certificate. The way out is to stop using `HostSNI("*")`: a **specific SNI** lets Traefik select the
  route's own `tls.secretName` certificate, with no default certificate touched and no new namespace. The
  client supplies the name via libpq's `host=` (which is what goes into the SNI, and what `verify-full`
  checks) together with `hostaddr=` (which is what is dialled). **No DNS record is created for those
  names**, deliberately: they are not meant to resolve, and `terraform/cloudflare.tf` forbids hand-written
  records outright ("adopt them with `cf-terraforming`, one record at a time"). The runbook now documents
  this, publishes the CA certificate, and keeps `verify-ca` as the minimum contract while recommending
  `verify-full` — which is now possible *because* there is a name to verify.
- **The edge CA and its leaves exist, with the CA key off-message.** RSA-2048 CA (`CA:TRUE`, keyCertSign,
  to 2036-09-26) signing one leaf per environment (EKU serverAuth, SANs `DNS:pg-<env>.upayan.dev` **and**
  `IP:138.201.157.147`, valid to 2028-09-28), both chain-verified with `openssl verify`. The leaves are in
  git only as SOPS secrets; the **CA key is not in either repository** — it lives in
  `~/.config/vps/vcap-pg-ca/` (0700, keys 0600) and belongs in the owner's password manager, which is the
  one step of this that still needs a human: **move `ca.key` there and delete the local copy**, or leaf

### 22:30 IST (2026-09-29) — P8-03 verified in both directions, and this commit is the proof of the second one

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** GitHub ruleset settings; this commit and the one before it.
  **Prod:** yes (branch protection on `main`).
- **Enforcement is live.** Ruleset 24126436 now carries `required_status_checks` for all seven contexts
  (`kustomize-and-schema`, `terraform`, `ansible`, `lint`, `secret-scan`, `secret-scan-history`,
  `redaction`) alongside its existing `deletion`, `required_linear_history` and `non_fast_forward`
  rules, with a single bypass actor of type `DeployKey`.
- **Direction 1 — normal changes are protected: measured.** A push to `main` with the ordinary
  credentials was **refused**: `! [remote rejected] p8-verify -> main (push declined due to repository
  rule violations)` / `7 of 7 required status checks are expected.` `origin/main` stayed at `43b466a`,
  so the attempt changed nothing. This is the same failure the owner's own token gets — the rule does
  not exempt admins, which is the point.
- **Direction 2 — the updater is not blocked: this commit proves it.** It was pushed over SSH with the
  `argocd-image-updater` deploy key (id 164843318), which is the only bypass actor. The same push with
  the ordinary credentials had just been refused, so the difference is the identity, not the timing.
  Note the honest caveat: GitHub **does not echo** `actor_id` back for a `DeployKey` bypass actor — the
  API returns `"actor_id": null` even when the actor is registered and working. A future reader must
  therefore test this functionally (push as the key) rather than by reading the ruleset, and should not
  "fix" the null.
- **What this does and does not yet prove.** It proves the ruleset accepts the updater's identity, that
  the deploy key can push, and that everything else is refused. It does **not** yet prove
  `argocd-image-updater` itself completing a write-back over SSH, because that only happens when a
  tracked image digest changes. Watch its first attempt: `kubectl -n argocd logs
  deploy/argocd-image-updater | grep -iE 'push|ssh|auth|denied'`. If that write-back fails, the fix is
  either the Secret's shape (`sshPrivateKey` / `insecure`) or removing the `required_status_checks`
  rule to restore the HTTPS+PAT path — both are single, reversible steps, and neither is a lockout
  because the ruleset is editable through the API regardless of what pushes are permitted.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 22:26 IST (2026-09-29) — P8-03: `main` gains the 7 required checks, with a deploy key as the only bypass (the updater's identity, because it cannot be the owner's)

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** new credential (repo deploy key + SOPS Secret in-cluster),
  annotation changes across 12 Applications, and a GitHub ruleset update. **Prod:** yes — the
  image-updater's write-back transport and the branch protection on the source of truth. **Owner
  decision:** chosen explicitly from three options, knowing the consequence below.
- **The measurement that shaped the whole change.** P8-03's CI row reads "ruleset 24126436 gains
  required checks (the 6 validate jobs + redaction job)". Before adding them, GitHub's actual behaviour
  was measured on this repository rather than assumed, and it does not permit the obvious version:
  | Probe | Result |
  |---|---|
  | push to a branch under an active `required_status_checks` rule, no bypass | **rejected** — `GH013 … Required status check "lint" is expected.` |
  | the same push made with the **owner's own token** | **rejected identically** — the rule does not spare admins |
  | `bypass_actors` with `DeployKey` | **accepted** by the API |
  | `bypass_actors` with `Integration` / `Team` / `OrganizationAdmin` | `Validation Failed` (user-owned repo) |
  | `RepositoryRole` | accepted, but that means "an admin", i.e. the owner — the opposite of the requirement |
  | a **write deploy key** listed as the bypass actor, push under the active rule | **accepted** — the branch landed |
  A required check cannot pass on a commit that does not exist yet, so *any* direct push to `main` is
  refused unless the pusher is a bypass actor. And the updater cannot simply be given that bypass:
  **it authenticates as the owner** — `argocd/git-creds` is a PAT that resolves to `upayanmazumder`
  (measured with `GET /user`) — so a bypass for the updater is a bypass for every manual push, which is
  exactly what the owner asked to avoid.
- **So the updater got its own identity: an SSH deploy key.** New write deploy key on this repository
  (**id 164843318**, `argocd-image-updater (P8-03 digest write-back)`), private half in
  `argocd/git-creds-updater` as a SOPS secret (`sshPrivateKey`, plus `insecure: "false"` for strict
  host-key verification — possible because the chart already mounts `argocd-ssh-known-hosts-cm` at
  `/app/config/ssh`, and github.com's keys are in it). Switching the *transport* to SSH was the smallest
  change that makes the identity distinct: a deploy key is per-repository and per-purpose, whereas the
  PAT is the owner.
- **Twelve Applications now write back over SSH**, and the split is deliberate:
  - the ApplicationSet template (10 apps: bandit, bitvault, cas-api, dj-html-nodejs-web, kodesphere,
    learning-docker, learning-qwik, learning-react, smart-home-system-api, upayan-web) —
    `git@github.com:upayanmazumder/cluster.git` via `git-creds-updater`;
  - `status-page` and `upayan-v5` likewise. **`upayan-v5` needed a second fix**: it had no
    `git-repository` annotation, so the updater derived the write target from the Application's
    `source.repoURL` (`https://…`), which cannot carry an SSH credential — it now names the SSH remote
    explicitly;
  - `meghmitra` stays on `write-back-method: argocd`, unchanged;
  - the three `vcap-*` apps keep the **PAT**, because they write to
    `vitscopesoftwareteam/VCAP-STAGING-*`. This cluster has no deploy key there and no say over those
    repositories' rulesets (that is P2-06, and the VCAP team's to accept).
  Prerequisites checked before relying on any of it: the updater's ServiceAccount can actually read the
  new Secret (`kubectl auth can-i get secrets/git-creds-updater` → **yes**), and the pod has `git`
  (`/usr/bin/git`) and `ssh` (`/usr/bin/ssh`).
- **Enforcement, and the consequence the owner accepted.** Ruleset 24126436 keeps its existing rules
  (`deletion`, `required_linear_history`, `non_fast_forward`) and gains `required_status_checks` for the
  seven real check-run names — `kustomize-and-schema`, `terraform`, `ansible`, `lint`, `secret-scan`,
  `secret-scan-history`, `redaction` (read from an actual commit's check runs, not from the job IDs in
  the workflow file) — with `bypass_actors` containing only the deploy key. **Effect: a human push to
  `main` is now refused**; changes land as a pull request, or by pushing a branch and then pushing that
  already-checked SHA to `main`. `.claude/CLAUDE.md` was updated in the same commit, because it
  documented `edit k8s/ → commit → git push origin main` and that instruction has stopped working.
  This is the trade the owner chose knowingly after the three options were laid out.
- **Verification, in order:** the bypass was proven with the *production* key (not a stand-in) by
  pushing to a probe branch under an active required-check rule — it landed; the probe branch, probe
  ruleset and any local clone were then deleted and the deletion re-checked (`rulesets` lists only
  24126436, `matching-refs/heads/p8probe` is 0, exactly one deploy key remains). The SOPS edit was
  verified to leave `ghcr-creds` byte-identical (hash comparison of the decrypted document before and
  after), the private/public halves match (`ssh-keygen -y`), and the live Secret carries
  `insecure=false` with a valid OpenSSH header.
- **Also done:** `gh workflow disable validate -R upayanmazumder/vps` → `disabled_manually`, the other
  half of P8-03's CI row.
- **What is still only argued, not observed** — recorded so it is not mistaken for verified: the
  updater's *own* write-back has not yet fired since the switch, because it only writes when a tracked
  image digest changes. The transport, the credential, the RBAC and the push capability are each proven;
  the remaining step is the updater exercising them on a real digest change. Until that happens, watch
  the updater's logs for an SSH/push error on its first attempt (`kubectl -n argocd logs
  deploy/argocd-image-updater | grep -i -E 'push|ssh|auth'`), and note the fallback: if SSH write-back
  misbehaves, removing the `required_status_checks` rule restores the previous HTTPS+PAT path in one
  `gh api` call.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** delete the bypass actor and the rule (push protection returns to
  today's state), or revert the annotations and remove the deploy key (write-back returns to the PAT).

### 22:20 IST (2026-09-29) — P7 network hardening: the tailnet becomes the admin path (N1, N2), Access goes live (N3), and 80/443 become Cloudflare-only (N4)

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** workstation config (`~/.ssh/config`, `~/.kube/config`), one
  Cloudflare Terraform apply, one Hetzner firewall apply, and this commit (`terraform/*`,
  `k8s/platform/argocd/values.yaml`, `k8s/monitoring/grafana-ingress.yaml`). **Prod:** yes — a second
  Cloudflare token, two Access applications, and the internet-facing firewall. Owner decisions recorded
  inline below.
- **N1 — the tailnet path works, verified three ways.** `tailscale status` shows `vps` online at
  `100.96.250.81`; `ssh vps` (after N2's config change) lands on the node; `kubectl --server
  https://100.96.250.81:6443 get nodes` returns the node without any k3s change, because the serving
  cert already carries that SAN. The plan's second admin device was waived by the owner, who accepted the
  single-device risk explicitly and asked that no gate be weakened for it.
- **N2 — daily tooling switched, and the lockout path proven before anything depends on it.**
  `~/.ssh/config` `Host vps` now points at `100.96.250.81` (the old public address is kept as
  `vps-public`), `~/.kube/config`'s single cluster moved to `https://100.96.250.81:6443` (a one-line
  diff; the backup is in `~/.config/omp-backups/kubeconfig.bak`), and IP-keyed `Host` blocks were added
  because **Ansible sets `ansible_host` from its inventory and therefore never matches the `vps` alias** —
  without them the playbook offered the wrong key and failed `Permission denied (publickey)` even though
  `ssh vps` worked. With that fixed, the plan's own verify passes over the tailnet:
  `ansible-playbook site.yml --check --tags backup` → `ok=22 changed=0 unreachable=0 failed=0`.
  The emergency path is proven, not assumed: `tf.sh plan -var emergency_ssh_cidr=182.156.30.27/32`
  reports exactly one resource changing (`hcloud_firewall.vps` in-place) and the only addition is
  `tcp/22` from that `/32`; nothing was applied. The owner enabled `tailscaled` at boot and logged into
  the Hetzner console — both were outstanding, and the first was the real defect: the unit was
  **disabled**, so a reboot would have brought the machine up with no tailnet and, after N5, no way in.
  N2's 24-hour observation window for N5 starts with this commit.
- **N3 — Cloudflare Access is live in front of both admin UIs, and it needed a second, narrower token.**
  Applied: 2 applications + 2 allow-policies for `argocd.upayan.dev` and `grafana.upayan.dev`
  (identity from the git-ignored `terraform/access.auto.tfvars`). Verified live —
  `curl -sI https://argocd.upayan.dev` → `HTTP/2 302`, `location: https://upayanmazumder.cloudflareaccess.com/cdn-cgi/access/login/argocd.upayan.dev…`,
  and the same for Grafana. Two things this entry should not let a reader forget:
  - **The first apply failed, and that is why there is a provider alias.** `Authentication error (10000)`.
    Measured rather than guessed: the token that Terraform had been using is valid and *active* yet has
    **no Access permission at all** — even a read of `/zones/<zone>/access/apps` is refused. The owner
    supplied a new token, and that one turned out to be **Access-only**: it reads Access, is refused on
    `/accounts/<id>/r2/buckets`, and answers `9109 Unauthorized` on zone settings — both of which this
    configuration manages. Replacing `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` with it would have left Terraform unable to
    refresh the two R2 buckets and the zone-settings override that still exist, whose failure mode is a
    false "must be recreated", not a clean error. So Access moved to a `cloudflare.access` provider alias
    (`TF_VAR_cloudflare_access_api_token`, in the SOPS secrets file) and the broad token kept the rest.
    Smaller blast radius by construction: the Access token cannot touch R2, DNS or zone settings.
  - **`web` was removed from both Ingresses, and it changes nothing — measured, not assumed.** Before the
    edit, a request straight to the origin on port 80 with the correct Host header already answered
    `HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found`, because `router.tls: "true"` makes Traefik build only a TLS router so the
    non-TLS entrypoint cannot match. The edit therefore removes a declaration that contradicted its own
    `tls: "true"`; it does not close a live plaintext path. (The first draft of the comment claimed it
    did. The measurement is what corrected it.)
- **N3 exposed a real hole, which is why N4 was not treated as merely "next in order".** With Access
  live, `curl -sk --resolve argocd.upayan.dev:443:138.201.157.147 https://argocd.upayan.dev` returned
  **`HTTP/2 200`** — the ArgoCD login page, straight from the origin, never passing through Access. The
  public DNS record makes finding that address trivial, so an edge-enforced policy was bypassable by
  anyone who used it. Access means nothing until the edge is the only way in, so N4 moved from "ordered
  after N3" to "required for N3 to have any effect".
- **N4 — 80/443 restricted to Cloudflare's ranges, after finding Hetzner's per-rule cap the hard way.**
  The first apply failed: `invalid input in fields 'rules[1].source_ips', 'rules[5].source_ips' …
  [value required to be smaller]`. It changed **nothing** — re-read afterwards, and the origin was still
  reachable, which is the one thing that mattered. Diagnosis, all of it on throwaway *unattached*
  firewalls (never the live one, never the server):
  | Probe | Result |
  |---|---|
  | 100 synthetic `/16`s in one rule | accepted |
  | the provider's real list truncated to **100** | accepted |
  | the same list truncated to **101** | **rejected** |
  | `ipv4(15) + ipv6(7) + china_ipv4(46) + china_ipv6(44) = 112` (the intended list) | rejected |
  | the same minus china-IPv6 = **68** | accepted |
  So the cap is exactly **100 CIDRs per rule**, and the intended list was 112. The fix drops
  `china_ipv6_cidr_blocks`, and it is a correction rather than a compromise: Cloudflare's own published
  `https://www.cloudflare.com/ips-v6-china` now returns an **empty** list, so the provider's 44
  China-IPv6 entries are ranges Cloudflare no longer advertises. China-IPv4 (46 entries, still published)
  **stays**, because Cloudflare's China network reaches origins from those and a silently dropped visitor
  looks exactly like a broken site. Every probe firewall was deleted; the API lists only `vps` again.
  Applied: `hcloud_firewall.vps` modified in place — 80 and 443 now carry 68 Cloudflare sources each,
  while 22, 6443, 5432, 5433, 41641 and icmp keep their original `0.0.0.0/0`/`::/0`. Verified after:
  from this workstation (not a Cloudflare range) port 443 and port 80 to `138.201.157.147` are
  **closed/filtered**, while `https://argocd.upayan.dev` still 302s to the Access login — which is
  itself proof that Cloudflare *can* still reach the origin, i.e. the range list is correct in both
  directions. `ssh vps` and `kubectl` are unaffected.
- **The 25-host sweep, re-measured after the fact, because the first run was misread.** First pass
  reported 8 hosts "NO RESPONSE". That was **this workstation**, not the cluster: curl tries Cloudflare's
  IPv6 first and this host has no IPv6 default route (`Could not connect to server … after 1 ms`). Forcing
  IPv4 (`curl -4`) gives **21 of 25 healthy** and exactly four failures — `qwik.dj.upayan.dev`,
  `react.dj.upayan.dev`, `api.ks.upayan.dev`, `api.react.dj.upayan.dev` — all two- or three-level names
  already recorded in `docs/certificates.md` as edge-TLS failures caused by Cloudflare Universal SSL
  covering one wildcard level, plus `api-bandit.upayan.dev`'s known 502. No host changed behaviour
  because of N4. Recorded because "8 hosts are down" and "my laptop has no IPv6" look identical in a
  sweep table, and only one of them is a migration finding.
- **Also verified while here:** P3-03 passes its own check live — the PVCs without `Delete=false` are
  exactly the plan's allowance (the two VCAP `*-data` and `meghmitra-data-pvc`, protected by their PVs'
  `Retain` + annotation, plus `smart-home-system-pvc` twice as class-none, plus meghmitra's legacy
  PVCs until P3-M6). And `meghmitra`'s standing `OutOfSync` is the intended design rather than drift:
  upstream `deploy/k8s` no longer declares `meghmitra-postgres-pvc`, ArgoCD marks it `requiresPruning`,
  and the sync reports `PruneSkipped: ignored (no prune)` because of that annotation. The soak subject
  will therefore never report `Synced` until P3-M6 removes the legacy PVC — read the P3-M5 gate as
  Healthy + 0 restarts, and do not "fix" it by pruning, which would delete the legacy PostGIS data.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** N4 is one variable (`cloudflare_only_ingress = false`) and one
  apply; N3 is the `enable_cloudflare_access` flag with `git revert` + apply; N2 is `~/.ssh/config` and
  `~/.kube/config` (backups taken). None of them require touching the node.

### 22:00 IST (2026-09-29) — `vps` is frozen against writes; and this is where new entries live from now on

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** GitHub repository settings on `upayanmazumder/vps` (ruleset
  `24192973`); no cluster or cloud resource touched. This entry exists in **this** repository because that is
  the change it records about: from the cutover, `cluster` is authoritative.
- **P6-07 was marked done on half its content.** It asked for a README banner **and** a `main` ruleset on
  `vps` blocking pushes. The banner shipped; the ruleset was never created — `vps` had **no ruleset at all**,
  so nothing prevented a write to the repository that is supposed to have stopped being an owner. Reopened
  and implemented.
- **Verified before, not assumed:** nothing writes to `vps` any more — no Application's
  `image-updater … git-repository` annotation names `vps` (P6-03 rewrote every one) and
  `git log --author=image-updater --since=<cutover>` on `vps` is **empty**. The freeze protects a property that
  already holds.
- **The ruleset, read back from the API:**
  ```
  id 24192973  "main freeze (archived source)"  target=branch  enforcement=active
  conditions: ref_name include ~DEFAULT_BRANCH
  rules:      update, deletion, non_fast_forward, required_linear_history
  bypass:     User 143063269 (upayanmazumder) — bypass_mode: always
  ```
  `update` blocks pushes; `deletion`/`non_fast_forward` protect the branch and its history; the **owner keeps
  `always` bypass** because the plan's wording is "blocking pushes except the owner's P8 tasks", and because
  **P8-05 still has to rename and archive this repository** — a freeze with no way out is a trap, not a control.
- **The bypass was exercised, and the first thing it produced was a misleading message.** The entry for this
  change was pushed to `vps` **after** the ruleset went active, and the remote printed:
  ```
  remote: - Cannot update this protected ref.
  To https://github.com/upayanmazumder/vps.git
     ba8bcb7..929245f  main -> main
  ```
  **The push succeeded** — `git ls-remote` and a fresh `fetch` both put `origin/main` at `929245f`. So that line
  is the ruleset's rule-evaluation message, emitted *alongside* an update the bypass permitted, not a
  rejection. It is recorded here because the next person to push to `vps` will see it and reasonably think the
  freeze is broken or their access is.
- **What was NOT tested, stated rather than implied:** that a **non-owner** actor is blocked. There is no
  second account or collaborator to push with, so the blocking half of this control is **UNVERIFIED**; what is
  verified is that the ruleset is `active` with an `update` rule and that the owner bypass works. Anyone
  wanting certainty can test it with a collaborator or a token belonging to another account.
- **Where entries go from here.** Post-cutover the authoritative repository is `cluster`, so **new changelog
  entries are written here** and `vps`'s changelog stops at this point — which is what an archive's record
  looks like. The previous practice (write in `vps`, mirror the redacted copy to `cluster`) would have meant a
  frozen repository still receiving commits, and it would have made `cluster`'s record a copy of a file being
  edited somewhere it is no longer supposed to change.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** `gh api -X DELETE repos/upayanmazumder/vps/rulesets/24192973`.
