### 21:50 IST (2026-09-29) — one Prometheus scrape target is dead, and half a fix would leave it dead

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** read-only investigation + chart rendering; **no change made**. This
  entry records the exact repair so it can be done in one pass, including the part that is easy to get wrong.
- **The defect.** Prometheus scrapes 15 targets; **one is down** — `up == 0` for job `argocd` at
  `argocd-repo-server.argocd.svc.cluster.local:8084`. The repo-server pod *does* listen on 8084
  (`containerPorts: {server: 8081, metrics: 8084}`), and `k8s/platform/argocd/network-policies/manifests.yaml`
  already allows 8084 from the `monitoring` namespace — the intent was clearly for this to work. What is
  missing is a route: the Service `argocd-repo-server` exposes **only** `tcp-repo-server 8081`, so the DNS name
  the scrape config uses has nothing on 8084.
- **The half-fix, and why it matters that this is written down.** The chart
  (`argo/argo-cd`, 7.9.1) has `repoServer.metrics.enabled` (default **false**), and it is tempting to set it
  and stop. **Rendered and checked: that alone does not fix the target.** With the flag on, Helm creates a
  *separate* Service:
  ```
  argocd-repo-server-metrics  ports: [('http-metrics', 8084, 'metrics')]
  argocd-repo-server          ports: [('tcp-repo-server', 8081, 'repo-server')]
  ```
  The scrape config would still point at `argocd-repo-server…:8084`, which still has no such port. The repair is
  **two coordinated edits**, not one:
  1. `k8s/platform/argocd/values.yaml` → `repoServer.metrics.enabled: true`;
  2. `k8s/monitoring/prometheus-configmap.yaml:85` → target
     `argocd-repo-server-metrics.argocd.svc.cluster.local:8084`.
  The existing NetworkPolicy needs no change: it selects pods and allows port 8084, and NetworkPolicies apply
  to pods rather than Services.
- **Verification to require after the repair:** `up{job="argocd"} == 1` (today it is `0`), and Prometheus's
  targets page showing 15/15 up.
- **Why not fixed here.** It is pre-existing, no plan task asks for it, and it is one line of a wider
  observability problem (the entry above: 18 of 24 alert rules fire permanently, and Loki's read path serves
  nothing). Repairing one scrape target while eighteen alerts still cry wolf changes none of the conclusions,
  and piecemeal edits to a monitoring stack are how the next person loses the thread. It belongs in a single,
  reviewed observability pass — with this entry as the specification.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** N/A.

### 21:45 IST (2026-09-29) — 18 of the 24 alert rules fire permanently on a healthy cluster: `noDataState: Alerting` meets "no series when all is well"

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** read-only investigation; **no rule was changed**. This entry exists
  because the repository's gates lean on alerting, and the alert set cannot currently support that.
- **How it surfaced.** Checking the cluster's health during the P6-05 soak, Grafana reported **19 rules
  `firing`** — on a cluster where every Application is `Synced`/`Healthy`, no pod is crash-looping, the node is
  Ready, the root filesystem is **72.9 %** used and the class-A backup had succeeded **11 minutes** earlier.
- **The mechanism, proven three ways rather than inferred.** Take `Node NotReady`, whose expression is
  `kube_node_status_condition{condition="Ready",status="true"} == 0`. Run that exact query against Prometheus:
  **NO DATA** — because the node *is* Ready, so no series satisfies `== 0`. The rule's `noDataState` is
  **`Alerting`**, and Grafana turns "no data" into **firing**. The same holds for `Root filesystem above 80 %
  full` (`(1 - avail/size) > 0.80` → 72.9 % means no series), `Pod in CrashLoopBackOff` (`== 1`, nothing
  crash-looping) and `TLS certificate expiring within 60 days` (`< 60`, certs a year out). The metric families
  all exist — this is not missing monitoring, it is rules whose *healthy* state is the absence of a series.
- **The correlation that settles it** (all 24 rules, from Grafana's authoritative rules-state API):

  | `noDataState` | rule state | count |
  |---|---|---|
  | `Alerting` | **firing** | **18** |
  | `OK` | firing | 1 |
  | `OK` | inactive | 5 |

  **Every** `Alerting` rule fires; no `OK` rule does — the one exception is `Low-priority app scaled up from
  zero`, which plausibly *should* fire. A perfect split across 24 rules is not coincidence, and it names the
  fix precisely.
- **What it means, and it is not "the alerts are noisy".** Eighteen of twenty-four rules are permanently
  firing, so **a genuine incident is indistinguishable from the standing background** — which is the whole
  value an alert set is supposed to provide. Concretely, the `VPS backup (class A) has not succeeded in over an
  hour` alert, which the incident earlier today *should* have been caught by and then cleared by, is still
  firing: measured staleness is **679 s** against a 2700 s threshold, so the expression is false and the firing
  is an artifact. **The class-A fix is verified by the service result and the R2 snapshot, not by that alert** —
  which is why the incident entry above cites `Result=success` and `744b509d`, and not a green alert list.
- **Two corrections to my own reading, recorded because they nearly became a wrong conclusion.**
  1. `GET /api/alertmanager/grafana/api/v2/alerts` returns **instances** (`status.state: active`), not "firing"
     rules — reading it as "25 firing" was wrong. The authoritative view is
     `/api/prometheus/grafana/api/v1/rules`.
  2. My first attempt to check the backup metrics queried `vps_backup_last_success_timestamp_seconds` — a name
     I supplied, not one that exists. The real names (no `_seconds`) are present and healthy. **A query that
     returns nothing is a hypothesis about the metric name, not evidence about the system.**
- **Why this is reported and not fixed.** Rewriting eighteen rules in one pass is exactly the kind of change
  that silences real alerts, it is pre-existing (none of these rules is from this migration), and the plan does
  not ask for it. The fix is one reviewable pass with a clear rule: **a threshold rule whose healthy state is
  "no series" must use `noDataState: OK`, and "I cannot see the metric" should be its own alert
  (`absent(...)`) rather than folded into the same condition** — conflating the two is what produced this.
  Worth noting for contrast: **both rules added today for N8 use `noDataState: OK` and are `inactive`**, the
  correct state, which is how the repair was confirmed empirically before being written down here.
- **Cost impact:** none. **Rollback:** N/A.

### 21:40 IST (2026-09-29) — backup RPO, measured: class-A is 30 min locally but up to ~60 min in R2

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** read-only verification; no change. This entry exists because a
  number the plan's gate depends on is not what the gate's wording implies, and someone should know that
  before relying on it.
- **What was measured, after the incident fixed earlier today:**
  - the class-A service is **succeeding on cadence** again — `Finished` at 14:53:56 (**`9a4ed15e`**) and at
    15:24:19 (**`74dede96`**), `Result=success`, `ExecMainStatus=0`, both backup timers `active`. The fix held.
  - the **offsite** leg is `OnBootSec=15min` + **`OnUnitActiveSec=1h`** — it re-bases one hour *after its last
    activation*, not on a wall-clock schedule. Recent activations 13:32, 13:59, 14:54, next due 15:54:46.
- **The consequence, stated plainly:** the local restic repository has a **~30-minute** class-A RPO, but the
  **R2 offsite copy has a ~60-minute** one, because it can only copy what the hourly run sees. So if the node
  is lost, up to about an hour of class-A data since the last offsite copy is gone. The plan's BACKUP gate —
  "R2 latest class-A < 30 min **at cutover**" — was satisfied when it mattered (I ran the offsite copy
  immediately before the cutover and measured `744b509d` at ~90 s old), but it is a **point-in-time** check,
  not a steady-state property. Reading it as "the R2 RPO is 30 minutes" would be wrong.
- **Why this is recorded and not changed:** the cadence is deliberate (the timer's own comment keeps it clear
  of class-A's 30-minute cycle), tightening it means more R2 traffic and cost, and it is the owner's call — not
  a defect to fix in passing. The honest position is that the numbers are now written down: **30 min local,
  ~60 min offsite**, with the offsite copy the one that survives losing the node.
- **A second red herring, recorded so it is not re-investigated:** `systemctl list-timers` showed the offsite
  timer's `LAST` as **12:58** — three hours before the check — which looks like a broken hourly timer. It is
  not: a manual `systemctl start` re-bases `OnUnitActiveSec`, so the listing and the journal disagree by
  design. The journal (13:32, 13:59, 14:54) is the truthful record here.
- **Cost impact:** none (read-only).
- **Rollback:** N/A.

### 21:35 IST (2026-09-29) — N4's firewall change is written and plan-proven, gated off; nothing applied

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit in `cluster` — `terraform/firewall.tf`,
  `terraform/variables.tf`. **No Terraform apply; the live Hetzner firewall is untouched.** This entry.
- **What it does.** N4 restricts the **80/443** rules to Cloudflare's published edge ranges, so the origin
  stops answering anyone who connects directly to `138.201.157.147`. Gated behind
  `cloudflare_only_ingress` (**default false**): with the flag off the expression is byte-identical to the
  `["0.0.0.0/0", "::/0"]` it replaces, so the applied firewall is provably unchanged.
- **Fetched, not hand-copied.** `data "cloudflare_ip_ranges" "cf"`, whose attributes were confirmed against
  the **pinned provider's own schema** (`terraform providers schema -json`, run with the backend credentials
  so it could initialise): `ipv4_cidr_blocks`, `ipv6_cidr_blocks`, `china_ipv4_cidr_blocks`,
  `china_ipv6_cidr_blocks`. A static list rots silently, and a wrong range list here is a total ingress
  outage.
- **The China-ranges decision, taken rather than deferred.** The old comment in this file asked for exactly
  this call to be made before the apply instead of discovered after it. **They are included.** Cloudflare's
  China network reaches origins from those ranges, so a rule built from the main lists alone silently drops
  that traffic — and a silently dropped visitor looks exactly like a broken site. Including them cannot break
  anyone; excluding them can. The measurement below shows the effect: 80/443 go from **2 ranges to 112**,
  which is materially more than the ~15 IPv4 + 7 IPv6 the main lists alone carry.
- **Only 80 and 443 are touched.** 22 and 6443 are deliberately left alone here: narrowing those is **N5**,
  and it depends on a working tailnet (N2), not on Cloudflare.
- **Verified, in both states, before committing:**

  | `cloudflare_only_ingress` | Result |
  |---|---|
  | false (default) | **`No changes. Your infrastructure matches the configuration.`** |
  | true | **`Plan: 0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`** — the firewall, and nothing else |

  And the one changed resource was inspected through the **plan's JSON**, not by eye, because the
  human-readable diff of a rule *set* is ambiguous:
  ```
  added  : 80/tcp  112 ranges      removed: 80/tcp   2 ranges
  added  : 443/tcp 112 ranges      removed: 443/tcp  2 ranges
  ```
  `22/tcp`, `6443/tcp`, `5432/tcp`, `5433/tcp`, `41641/udp` and the `icmp` rule are **unchanged**. One caution
  worth recording: the first pass *appeared* to show the icmp rule changing, which was an artifact of my own
  comparison — Terraform represents an unset optional `port` as `""` coming from state and as absent coming
  from config, so keying on it produced two phantom rows. Comparing by protocol and ranges settles it.
- **Also verified:** `terraform fmt -check -recursive` clean; `terraform validate` clean on both the local
  1.15 **and the pinned 1.7.5** (isolated copy, the way CI runs it — the lesson from the entry above applied
  rather than repeated).
- **What N4 still needs:** `terraform apply` — and the plan's sequencing puts it after N3 is *verified*, since
  N4 makes Cloudflare the only path to the admin UIs. Applying it is not done here.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** set `cloudflare_only_ingress = false` and apply; the 80/443 rules revert to the world.

### 21:28 IST (2026-09-29) — the cause of the repeated Terraform failure: CI's one unpinned tool

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit in `cluster` — `.github/workflows/validate.yml`,
  new `terraform/.terraform-version`, `terraform/README.md`. No infrastructure touched. This entry.
- **The change.** CI's `terraform_version` was `"~> 1.7"` — **the only range in a workflow whose stated
  principle (CI-007) is "pinned so local runs and CI agree"**, next to shellcheck `0.11.0`, tflint `0.64.0`,
  gitleaks `v8.30.1`, yamllint `1.38.0` and ansible-lint `26.9.0`, all exact. It is now `"1.7.5"`, and
  `terraform/.terraform-version` carries the same value so a version manager picks it up locally.
- **Why this is the fix and not a tidy-up.** The range is precisely what let the same bug through twice
  (`2500dc9` on P3-04, `1f73356` on N3): "`~> 1.7`" resolved to 1.7.5 in CI while the workstation ran 1.15.9,
  and 1.15 accepts a cross-variable `variable` validation that 1.7 rejects. Fixing the check twice without
  fixing the drift guarantees a third occurrence — most likely in N7, which is where the next cross-variable
  check will plausibly be written.
- **`terraform/README.md`** now carries the two-incident table above, the rule it implies ("a local
  `terraform validate` is evidence for the version that ran it and for nothing else"), and the four-command
  recipe to run the pinned version before pushing.
- **Verified:** the workflow still parses (`yaml.safe_load` → the `terraform` job keeps its 7 steps);
  `terraform fmt -check -recursive` clean; `terraform plan` still `No changes` on the default tree;
  `check-links.py` 179/179.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert; a range and an exact pin behave identically in CI today (both resolve to 1.7.5), so
  reverting only reintroduces the drift.

### 21:20 IST (2026-09-29) — I reproduced the exact Terraform bug this repository had already fixed, and CI caught it again

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** fix to `terraform/access.tf` + `terraform/variables.tf` (commits in
  `cluster`), plus this entry. **New `cluster` commit for the N3 work; that is the one production change.**
- **What happened.** The N3 commit (`f986e53`) turned the `terraform` CI job red — the whole job, at
  `terraform init`:
  ```
  Error: Invalid reference in variable validation
    on variables.tf line 38, in variable "enable_cloudflare_access":
      condition = !var.enable_cloudflare_access || length(var.access_allowed_emails) > 0
  The condition for variable "enable_cloudflare_access" can only refer to the variable itself.
  ```
- **This is a repeat, and the honest part of this entry is that it is a repeat.** The identical mistake was made
  and fixed on P3-04 (`2500dc9`, the `enable_rebuild_server` auth-key check) — the same shape: a
  **`variable` `validation` that references a second variable**, which Terraform rejects because a variable
  validation may only refer to its own variable. Both times the guard was about two variables agreeing; both
  times the local toolchain said the configuration was fine.
- **Why the local checks kept lying, restated because it clearly needs repeating:** the workstation runs
  Terraform **1.15.9**, which accepts the cross-variable reference; CI pins **`~> 1.7`** (1.7.5), which rejects
  it. `terraform fmt -check`, `terraform validate` and three `terraform plan` runs all passed locally against a
  configuration CI could not initialise. **A local `validate` is evidence for the version that ran it and for
  nothing else** — and that sentence is already in this changelog, which is exactly why repeating the bug is
  worth recording rather than quietly fixing. The previous fix moved the check to a `lifecycle.precondition`;
  this one does the same, in `access.tf`.
- **The fix, and its semantics, which are not identical to a validation.** The guard is now a
  `lifecycle.precondition` on `cloudflare_zero_trust_access_application.admin_ui`:
  `length(var.access_allowed_emails) > 0`. It can reference both variables, and it is evaluated **only when the
  resource is planned** — so with the feature off, `for_each` is empty, nothing is evaluated, and the default
  path stays inert. `variables.tf` carries a pointer explaining why the check lives in the other file, so the
  next person does not "tidy" it back.
- **Verified against the pinned version, not the local one:**
  - Terraform **1.7.5** (the CI pin) in an isolated copy, as CI runs it: `init -backend=false` → *successfully
    initialized*; `validate` → **Success!**, no warnings.
  - `terraform fmt -check -recursive` clean.
  - the three behavioural states, re-run after the change: flag off → **`No changes`**; flag on with an empty
    allow-list → **`Error: Resource precondition failed`** (the guard fires at plan time, before anything is
    created); flag on with one placeholder identity → **`Plan: 4 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`**.
- **Cost impact:** none — nothing was applied.
- **Rollback:** revert the change; the resources are gated off by default either way.

  | `enable_cloudflare_access` | `access_allowed_emails` | `terraform plan` |

  | true | `[]` | **refused** — the validation fires at plan time, before anything is created |
  | true | one placeholder identity | **`Plan: 4 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`** (2 applications + 2 policies) |

  Also: `terraform fmt -check -recursive` clean and `terraform validate` **clean with no warnings** (which is
  what the renamed resources bought us).
- **What N3 still needs, in order:** (1) the owner supplies the identities out-of-band into a git-ignored

### 21:12 IST (2026-09-29) — the stored Cloudflare token is broader than this repo claims (it can write Access: N3 needs no new token)

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** read-only capability probe against the live Cloudflare API; one
  published file corrected. No Cloudflare resource was created, changed or deleted.
- **What was measured, and why it is worth an entry.** The repository states the workstation's Cloudflare API
  token is *SSL and Certificates: Edit* (`CLAUDE.md`, and `docs/plans/public-cluster-migration.md`). A probe
  says otherwise:
  - `GET /zones/<zone>/access/apps` → **`success: true`** (Access **read**);
  - `POST /zones/<zone>/access/apps` with a deliberately **invalid** body → **`12130
    access.api.error.invalid_request: app type is missing or invalid`** — a *validation* error. A missing
    scope answers **`9109 Unauthorized to access requested resource`**, which is what `/accounts` returns. So
    the token holds Access **write** as well.
  - **The probe cannot create anything by construction:** an empty body fails validation before any resource
    is considered, so this carries no side effect. That is the point of probing capability with an invalid
    payload rather than a plausible one.
- **Two consequences, one operational and one not:**
  1. **N3's blocker is smaller than recorded.** Writing `cloudflare_access_application` +
     `cloudflare_access_policy` for `argocd`/`grafana` needs **no new token** — it is already possible. The
     only missing input is the **allow-list of identities** (the plan's private tfvars value, TF-003), which
     is the owner's to supply and must never be printed or committed. The Terraform itself does not exist yet
     (`grep cloudflare_access terraform/*.tf` → nothing), so N3 is "write it, then supply one variable".
  2. **A credential is broader than its documentation.** A token documented as certificate-only can rewrite
     the zone's Access policies — that is the difference between a credential that can issue a certificate and
     one that can change who may reach the control plane's front door. Either narrow it to the scopes actually
     needed, or record the wider scope deliberately; what must not stand is the description saying
     "certificates only". `.claude/CLAUDE.md` — a **published** file — now states the measured scope and both
     consequences.
- **Not claimed:** the full permission set was **not** enumerated (`/user/tokens/permission_groups` is
  itself `9109`-denied for this token). What is claimed is exactly the three probes above, and no more.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert the `CLAUDE.md` paragraph; nothing else changed.

### 21:08 IST (2026-09-29) — Promtail does not collect the host journal; and the experiment's namespace wedged in `Terminating` the way this repo documents

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** READ-ONLY inspection of the live DaemonSet and the repo config, plus one
  permitted break-glass finalize of a namespace the experiment created. This entry.
- **Promtail and the host journal — the plan's last cheap UNVERIFIED item, now resolved.** The plan asked whether
  Promtail ingests the host journal. **It does not.** `k8s/monitoring/promtail-config.yaml` has exactly **one**
  scrape config, `kubernetes-pods`, globbing `/var/log/containers/*.log`; there is no `journal:` block anywhere. The
  live DaemonSet's volumes confirm the pod *could*: `varlog` is a `hostPath` on `/var/log`, and the node has
  `/var/log/journal` with `systemd-journald` **active**. So this is a **missing scrape config, not a missing mount** —
  the distinction matters because it is the difference between a one-block change and a volume redesign.
  **Consequence:** no alert can be built on host-level logs (sshd, kernel, systemd) as things stand. After N5 sshd is
  tailnet-only, so the blast radius stays informational — which is why it is recorded rather than fixed here. It does
  **not** change N8's two rules: both read container logs, which Promtail does ship.
- **And a blunt caveat, because it would be easy to draw the wrong conclusion from the paragraph above:** none of
  N8's log-based alerting works today for a *different* reason — Loki returns zero series for every selector (entry
  below). Adding a journal scrape would ship logs into a store that cannot read them back. Repairing Loki comes
  first.
- **The experiment's namespace wedged in `Terminating`, and it is the exact condition this repository documents.**
  After the TLS probe was deleted, `tls-probe` sat `Terminating` for 18 minutes with:
  `NamespaceDeletionDiscoveryFailure … external.metrics.k8s.io/v1beta1: stale GroupVersion discovery`, alongside
  `ContentRemoved` and `ContentHasNoFinalizers` and `spec.finalizers: ['kubernetes']`. That is the `NamespaceDeletion
  DiscoveryFailure` entry in `.claude/CLAUDE.md` **recurring**, caused by KEDA's aggregated metrics APIService being
  briefly unreachable when the namespace controller tried to enumerate API groups — a stale-discovery failure, not an
  unreleased finalizer.
  **Handled by the documented procedure, in the documented order:** the namespace was first *proved empty*
  (0 pods/services/configmaps/secrets/deployments/endpoints, no PV or PVC referencing it), and only then were
  `.spec.finalizers` emptied through the `/finalize` subresource —
  `kubectl replace --raw /api/v1/namespaces/tls-probe/finalize -f -` (**BREAK-GLASS EXCEPTION**, logged here as the
  only record it gets). Verified afterwards: the namespace is `NotFound`, the namespace count is back to **25**, the
  same number recorded in the P0-01 baseline, and `v1beta1.external.metrics.k8s.io` reports
  `Available=True — passed all checks` again, i.e. the stale discovery was transient rather than a broken KEDA.
- **Cleanup verified:** no `tls-probe`/`pg-probe`/`traefik-probe` object remains cluster-wide; `argocd.upayan.dev`
  and `meghmitra.upayan.dev` both still answer **200**; node load and disk are unchanged.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** N/A.

### 21:06 IST (2026-09-29) — experiment: Traefik **can** terminate TLS for Postgres, but only with a default certificate — the plan's UNVERIFIED TLS item, resolved

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** throwaway in-cluster experiment in a namespace created and deleted
  the same hour; the plan file updated with the result. **No production object was created, changed or
  exposed** — ClusterIP only, no public port, no VCAP resource touched.
- **Why it was worth doing:** N7's entire TLS design rests on the claim "Traefik terminates PostgreSQL
  STARTTLS … **UNVERIFIED** until tested: Traefik STARTTLS with `HostSNI("*")` for clients that send no SNI
  (libpq sends none when connecting by IP)". If it were false, the whole approach for 15432/15433 would need
  redesigning — and it would have been discovered while opening a public database, which is the worst
  possible time. The plan itself said to resolve UNVERIFIED items rather than guess.
- **Setup, faithful to the target:** a `postgres:16-alpine` pod with **`ssl = off`** (exactly VCAP's state),
  the **live Traefik image** `rancher/mirrored-library-traefik:3.6.7`, a TCP router on `:15432` with
  `rule: HostSNI("*")` and `tls: {}`, and a private CA (`basicConstraints: critical, CA:TRUE`) signing a leaf
  with `extendedKeyUsage = serverAuth` and a SAN. Clients were ordinary `psql` (PostgreSQL 16.14) in the same
  namespace, dialling a ClusterIP service — **no SNI**, the case in question.
- **Measured results:**

| Client mode | Result |
|---|---|
| `sslmode=require` | **1 row** — TLS terminates at Traefik and the Postgres session completes through it |
| `sslmode=verify-ca`, correct CA | **1 row** — the plan's chosen client mode works |
| `sslmode=verify-ca`, **wrong** CA | `certificate verify failed` — verification is real |
| `sslmode=disable` (plaintext) | refused at the router — the plan's requirement holds |
| control: direct to Postgres `:5432`, plaintext | 1 row — Postgres itself is untouched; the refusal is Traefik's |

- **The finding that changes the implementation.** With only a `tls.certificates` entry, Traefik logs
  `Serving default certificate for request: "…"` and serves **its own self-signed certificate** for
  `HostSNI("*")` — the leaf is never presented, and every verifying client fails with a message
  (`certificate verify failed`) that blames the certificate rather than the missing default. Installing the
  leaf as the **entrypoint's default certificate** (`tls.stores.default.defaultCertificate`) is what makes
  `verify-ca` succeed. **So N7 needs `tls.secretName` *and* a `TLSStore`**; the plan's N7 step and its TLS row
  have been corrected in place, with the measurement recorded beside them rather than left as a footnote.
  Two smaller notes carried into N7: a leaf without `basicConstraints: critical, CA:FALSE` and
  `extendedKeyUsage = serverAuth` is rejected by strict verifiers regardless of the chain (my first probe PKI
  made exactly that mistake and produced a misleading failure), and `verify-full` only works when the dialled
  host is in the SAN — which is why the plan's choice of `verify-ca` for IP-dialled clients is right.
- **What is still NOT proven, stated plainly:** that the same behaviour holds for a client **outside** the
  cluster, across the Hetzner firewall and the `svclb` LoadBalancer. The experiment proves the Traefik path;
  it does not prove the public path, and it must not be read as if it did. That is N7's own verification.
- **Cleanup verified:** the namespace is deleted, no `tls-probe`/`pg-probe`/`traefik-probe` object remains
  anywhere in the cluster, and the hosts were re-checked healthy afterwards.
- **Cost impact:** none (two short-lived pods, deleted).
- **Rollback:** N/A — nothing persisted except the plan and this entry.

### 21:05 IST (2026-09-29) — N8 authored; **Loki's read path is broken**; and P8-03's CI step would break the image write-back

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commits in `cluster` (the alert rules; both pushes), plus this
  entry. No live object outside `monitoring` changed.
- **N8 (P7) — the two auth-failure alerts are provisioned.** `vcap-postgres-auth-failures` (LogQL, > 50 in
  10 m, `severity: critical`) and `argocd-login-failures` (> 10 in 10 m, `severity: warning`), both in the
  `cluster-health` group and both confirmed present by Grafana's provisioning API
  (`/api/v1/provisioning/alert-rules` → 25 rules, both uids listed). Log-based deliberately: an auth failure
  leaves no metric behind, and the only record of "the superuser was refused over the network" is the
  server's own log line.
- **Both are inert, and the cause is not the rules.** `argocd-login-failures` **was** exercised — three
  deliberate wrong-password logins against `argocd.upayan.dev` (`Invalid username or password`, confirmed in
  the CLI) — and then its exact LogQL run straight against Loki returned **zero series**. Loki's own querier
  log for that query is the proof it is not the query:
  ```
  status=200 returned_lines=0 store_chunks_download_time=0s chunk_refs_fetch_time=425µs
  table_manager: "query readiness setup completed" distinct_users_len=0
  ```
  The **store returns no chunk references for any selector, at any range** (a 7-day query behaves the same),
  and the index reports no tenants. The write path is healthy: `loki_distributor_bytes_received_total
  {tenant="fake"} = 522 MB`, `loki_ingester_chunks_created_total = 4804`, 12 020 files under `/loki/chunks`,
  98 index periods on disk, Promtail tailing. Config: one schema, `from: 2020-10-24`, `boltdb-shipper` +
  `filesystem`, `index.period: 24h`.
  **Consequence, stated because it is bigger than N8:** everything log-derived is inert — the VCAP log
  dashboards (`vcap-logs.json`), and any future alert that reads Loki. `prometheus`-backed rules are
  unaffected, which is why the rest of the rule set looks healthy.
  **Blast radius of a fix, and why it stops here:** correcting Loki's index/table resolution is its own task,
  is **pre-existing** (the pod is 43 h old, Promtail 40 d, the chunk files date from July), and is unrelated
  to this migration. Nothing in this session touched Loki. A careless storage change could destroy the only
  copy of the log history, so it is recorded rather than attempted.
- **P8-03's CI step, not applied, with the reason.** The plan asks for the `cluster` ruleset to gain the six
  validate jobs plus `redaction` as **required status checks**. Measured against the ruleset's current shape
  (`[deletion, required_linear_history, non_fast_forward]`, `bypass_actors: []`): required status checks apply
  to **pushes**, not only to merges, so adding them with an empty bypass list would make **every direct push
  to `main` fail until the checks have run on that commit — which is impossible for a commit that does not
  exist yet.** The victim is not hypothetical: `argocd-image-updater` writes digest pins by **pushing directly
  to `main`** (`write-back-method: git`, credential `argocd/git-creds`), so this change would silently break
  the write-back — and the soak is exactly when that path matters. Making it safe requires a decision this
  session should not take on the owner's behalf: add the owner's account (and/or the updater's identity) to
  `bypass_actors`, **or** leave required checks off and treat CI as advisory. Recorded as a decision, not a
  task to be guessed at.
- **What P8-03 can already be ticked off:** Terraform ownership (P3-06/P3-07 — no `.tf` in `vps`,
  `state_owner` = `cluster`), image-updater (P6-06), the redaction gate (P5-04, live and green in CI), the
  Terraform secrets' new location (P5-05), and the tenant apps' ownership (P3-M4). `gh workflow disable
  validate -R upayanmazumder/vps` was **not** run: it is a one-way-looking change to the other repository and
  `vps` is not archived yet (P8-05), so its CI still has a job to do if the archive needs a fix.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert the alert-rules commit; the two rules disappear at the next monitoring sync.

### 20:40 IST (2026-09-29) — P4-08: the ArgoCD bootstrap secret is gone, and the two orphaned CSI volumes are protected

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** one break-glass `kubectl delete`, two Hetzner API calls
  (break-glass by nature: those volumes are not in Terraform). This entry — their only audit trail.
- **`argocd/argocd-initial-admin-secret` deleted.** ArgoCD's server validates against
  `argocd-secret`'s `admin.password` (the bootstrap Secret is a first-install artifact it never reads
  again), and `argocd-secret` carries that hash — so the login already in use *was* the proof. Verified
  after deletion: `argocd account get-user-info` → `Logged In: true / Username: admin`, and the Secret is
  `NotFound`. Removing it takes a second, plaintext copy of the admin password out of the cluster's
  namespace (anyone with read on `argocd` could have read it).
- **Hetzner delete protection enabled on `106574315` and `106574316`** — verified
  `protection.delete=true`, alongside `106969476` (`vps-data`, already protected).
  **What those two volumes actually are, since the plan called them "detached" and the labels say more:**
  `vcap-backend-dev-data` and `vcap-backend-staging-data` — the **old** vcap Postgres data volumes from
  before the static-PV migration to `/srv/data`. `server: null` in the API listing means "not attached as
  a server volume", not "abandoned": they carry the previous copies of the VCAP datasets, nothing is
  reading them, and a `hetzner-csi` reconcile could delete them. That is exactly why they are protected.
  **Consequence to remember:** the protection has **no expiry** — the plan's "until 2026-10-12" means
  someone must deliberately disable it (`POST /volumes/<id>/actions/change_protection {"delete": false}`)
  once the owner has decided the old data is no longer needed. Leaving it on forever is the safer error.
- **Cost note:** the two protected volumes keep being billed (10 GB each) for as long as they exist.
  Protection prevents accidental deletion; it does not stop a deliberate one, and it does not stop the
  charge. Their disposition is an owner decision, not a cleanup task.
- **Method note, cheap to carry forward:** `PUT /volumes/{id}` with `delete_protection: true` **silently
  does nothing** — the API returns the volume unchanged and no error. The right call is
  `POST /volumes/{id}/actions/change_protection` with `{"delete": true}`. The first attempt here used the
  wrong one and was caught by verifying the result instead of trusting the 200.

  **replicas 0**.

  does not implement `\b`** — so the command was a **silent no-op** (measured: 38 references before, 38
  after). Re-run without the boundary: 38 → 1, and the last one
  (`scripts/helm-template-all.sh`, which matched a string without the `https://github.com/` prefix) was
  fixed by hand. `vps` was **re-added** to each project's `sourceRepos` so R-1's rollback stays available

  Root's `spec.source.repoURL` went from `…/vps` to `…/cluster` and resolved `eeba6a0`; the sync
  **Succeeded**.

  - **nothing was deleted.** Every Application's current operation was inspected for `Pruned` resources:
    **NONE** in the window. The one prune this check did surface (`hetzner-csi Secret/hcloud-csi`) belongs
    to an operation from **2026-09-28T21:06:14Z** — a stale status entry, not this sync.

  two-level TLS hosts `000` at the Cloudflare edge).

  behaviour: the updater now logs `considering 18 annotated application(s)`, updates `rankstack`'s live spec

  commit into `cluster` cannot be forced on demand** (it needs a genuine digest change), so that half is

  class of gap as the Terraform version drift: **the linter that caught it cannot be run from this

- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** R-1, valid while `cluster` and `vps` still carry the same content. It requires
  `vps/k8s/bootstrap/root-app.yaml` (with `automated` removed), and it is now **asymmetric** — `cluster`
  has commits `vps` does not (the P6-03 rewrite, the incident fix, the CI fixes), so a rollback would revert
  those. Called out rather than assumed.

### 20:25 IST (2026-09-29) — incident: class-A backups had been failing for ~50 minutes, because a backup target named a workload P3-M4 pruned

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit (`ansible/files/backup-targets.yaml`,
  `ansible/roles/backup/templates/vps-backup.service.j2`, `k8s/monitoring/grafana-alerting-rules.yaml`) plus
  an Ansible run on the node. **The P3-M6 repo half, pulled forward because this was an active failure.**
- **How it was found:** the P6-04 preflight requires a class-A snapshot younger than 30 minutes.
  `systemctl start vps-backup-classA` **failed**, and the newest class-A snapshot was `197372cf` at 13:59Z —
  ~50 minutes stale. Not a retry-until-it-works situation; the journal named the cause:
  ```
  FAILED: meghmitra/meghmitra-postgres (postgres)
  ```
- **Root cause:** `ansible/files/backup-targets.yaml` still listed the **legacy** meghmitra Postgre as a
  **class-A** target. P3-M4 pruned that Deployment when the application on those hostnames was replaced, so
  the dump has nowhere to run — and the script sets `overall_rc=1`, which fails the **whole class-A run**,
  not just that target. So from ~21:35 IST the `vps-backup-classA` timer had been failing every 30 minutes
  and **vcap-staging's class A data (the student data) was not being backed up at all.**
- **The lesson, which is the reason this is written down:** a backup target whose workload has been removed
  does not "back up nothing" — it takes every dataset beside it out of coverage and turns the alert into a
  wall of noise. The plan scheduled this removal for P3-M6, *after* the P3-M5 soak; the ordering was wrong,
  and the failure was silent for the length of that window.
- **Fix:**
  - the dead `meghmitra/meghmitra-postgres` target is **removed**, with the reason recorded in the file;
  - the replacement's store is **added as a class-C path target** (`/srv/data/meghmitra-data`, OD-14), with
    `sqlite: vivaad.db` so the copy is taken with SQLite's own `.backup` API rather than read live — the app
    appends an AuditLog row per request, so a live read is a torn artifact that looks like a backup. `sqlite3`
    is present on the node (`/usr/bin/sqlite3`), so the step actually runs rather than warning;
  - the class-A alert summary and the systemd unit description no longer name a workload that does not exist
    (`No successful class-A backup (vcap-staging Postgres+MinIO) …`).
- **Applied and verified:** `ansible-playbook site.yml --tags backup` (the tag exists thanks to P4-06) →
  `changed=3 failed=0`. Then `systemctl start vps-backup-classA` → **rc=0**, `vps-backup-classC` → **rc=0**,
  and the offsite leg → **rc=0**. The gate the preflight asks for, measured in **R2** (which is what the
  offsite leg copies to, and which the earlier checks in this session were already reading):
  **newest class-A `744b509d` @ 2026-09-29T14:53:55Z** — about ninety seconds old, against a 30-minute
  requirement — and newest class-C `217dfc9e` @ 14:54:13Z.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert; the legacy target cannot succeed anyway while its Deployment is pruned. Restoring the
  old PostGIS app (R-9) also means restoring its target.

### 20:20 IST (2026-09-29) — P5-01: `cluster` re-seeded as one clean commit; its history is now publishable

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** DESTRUCTIVE history rewrite of the private `cluster` repository
  (approved: OD-6), plus a one-time ruleset bypass. This entry.
- **Why:** the root commit `804db0e` carried a literal account password in `changelog/2026-08.md` and two
  VCAP developers' names in `inventory/ports.yaml`. Deleting them from the tree would not have removed them
  from history — and history is what gets published (D-5 publishes the changelog, D-8 publishes `.claude/`).
- **What was done:**
  1. built the tree from the export (`export-public.sh`, redaction gate clean) and **overlaid the paths that
     live only in `cluster`** — `terraform/` (the configuration moved there at P3-04/P3-06, so the export can
     no longer carry it), `.github/workflows/validate.yml` (it keeps the `terraform` job), `.gitignore` (a
     superset) and `scripts/tf.sh`. **This is a correction to the plan's P5-01**, which takes the seed as the
     whole tree: as written it would have deleted the Terraform configuration from the public repository at
     the moment that repository became its owner. Found by diffing the seed against `cluster` before pushing
     — the two differed by exactly the changelog (expected) and nothing else, so the overlay is complete and
     provable.
  2. `git checkout --orphan seed`, `git rm -rq .`, copy the tree, one commit:
     **`742170d` — "Seed: cluster GitOps repository"**, author and committer
     `upayanmazumder <143063269+upayanmazumder@users.noreply.github.com>` (the GitHub noreply identity;
     `143063269` is the account id, so no personal address is in the history), **338 files**.
  3. the one-time bypass the plan describes: ruleset `24126436` was read, `non_fast_forward` dropped from a
     copy, `git push --force origin seed:main`, then the **exact original** ruleset restored —
     `[deletion, required_linear_history, non_fast_forward]` verified identical afterwards, `bypass_actors`
     empty both times.
  4. the checkout was replaced: `cluster` moved to **`cluster.pre-reseed`** (the only remaining copy of the
     three old commits — never re-push it, delete it after P6-05) and a fresh clone took its place.
- **Verification — the publication proofs, run on the seed commit before the push:**
  - `gitleaks git --all` with the repo config → **no leaks**; with the default rule set only → **no leaks**;
    `gitleaks dir` default-only → **no leaks**;
  - `scripts/check-secrets.sh` → OK; `scripts/check-links.py` → 177/177; README, LICENSE and SECURITY.md present;
  - `git ls-files` for `.tfstate|.pem|.key|.env|kubeconfig|secrets.enc.env` → **0**; `git ls-files
    k8s/apps/meghmitra` → **0**; `terraform/*.tf` → 12 files present; `scripts/tf.sh` executable;
    `validate.yml` carries both the `terraform` and the `redaction` job;
  - every one of the 17 redaction terms, searched individually against the seed commit with `git grep`:
    **none present**;
  - after the push: `gh api …/commits` → **1**; `git rev-list --count HEAD` → **1**;
    `git rev-list --max-parents=0 HEAD` = HEAD; `git log --format='%ae%ce' | sort -u` → the noreply address
    only; `git ls-remote --heads` → `main` only.
  - **the gate that had been failing on the old history now passes on the real thing:** in a fresh clone,
    `scripts/check-redaction.sh --history` → `clean (17 terms, fixed-string, tree + history)`, having scanned
    **1** commit. CI run `36584387158` on `742170d`: `redaction`, `secret-scan`, `secret-scan-history`,
    `kustomize-and-schema`, `terraform` and `lint` all **success**.
- **The honest limitation, unchanged from the plan:** the old commits' *objects* were the reason for this
  rewrite, and unreachable objects can persist in GitHub's object store until it garbage-collects them. They
  are no longer reachable by any ref (nothing points at them), which is what makes the published history
  clean — but this is a history rewrite, not a rotation, and OD-9 declines rotating the old-history
  credentials at this stage.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** `cluster.pre-reseed` holds the pre-rewrite state; the plan's R-11 says a second corrected
  orphan commit may be pushed **while `cluster` is still private**, and that after P6-04 history rewrites are
  no longer permitted.

### 23:40 IST (2026-09-29) — P5-02/P5-03/P5-04: two classes the gate could not see, a scan that proved nothing, and a gate that outlives `vps`

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit — `.gitleaks.toml`, `.pre-commit-config.yaml`,
  `scripts/check-secrets.sh`, new `scripts/check-redaction.sh`, `scripts/private/check-public-redaction.sh`,
  `.github/workflows/validate.yml`, `inventory/ports.yaml`, `.claude/CLAUDE.md`. Plus an **Actions secret**
  and a live `chmod`. This entry.
- **The headline finding: two personal names and a workstation path would have been published.** The export
  reported **clean** while carrying `inventory/ports.yaml:98,128` (VCAP's collaborators, named in an owner
  note) and `.claude/CLAUDE.md:122` (the owner's token path). Both had been *listed in the redaction
  checklist as prose* — and a rule with no pattern is a rule that has not been implemented. This is the same
  failure the four personal addresses had, so it is recorded the same way. Fixed at source (names → roles;
  the path → "stored outside both repositories"), and the gate gained patterns for both classes, which were
  each then **proved to fire** against a planted file before being relied on.
- **P5-03, a suppression that hid the thing it was for.** `.gitleaks.toml` carried a path allowlist for
  `secrets\.sops\.yaml$` — i.e. it skipped exactly the files whose *name* claims to be encrypted. Measured:
  with `useDefault = true` and **no** allowlist, `gitleaks dir` finds **no leaks** in this tree, so the
  allowlist was doing nothing except suppressing. It is gone, and `scripts/check-secrets.sh` now asserts the
  ciphertext itself: every `data`/`stringData` leaf of a `k8s/…` SOPS file, and every leaf of a full-value
  SOPS file, must be `ENC[…]`. Empty values are skipped, consistent with the existing rule — an absent value
  is not a leaked one. Verified both ways: clean on the tree (17 SOPS files), and a planted plaintext leaf
  fails it. The pre-commit hook now scans **the staged diff** (`git --pre-commit --staged`) rather than the
  whole history, which on this repository's history could never pass — a hook that always fails is one
  everyone disables.
- **P5-04, the dead check.** The private gate's `age key path (bare)` check used a PCRE lookbehind
  (`(?<![./])…`) with `grep -E`. GNU grep does not support it; it prints
  `warning: ? at start of expression` and exits **1**, which the guard read as "no matches" — so the check
  matched nothing and said nothing, and a bare `sops' default age-key location` would have exported clean. Two
  fixes, because either alone leaves the hole: the pattern is now valid ERE (`(^|[^./])…`), and **any
  diagnostic output is treated as an ERROR even when grep exits 1**. Both controls were run: a home-prefixed
  path and a bare one each fail the gate.
- **New `scripts/check-redaction.sh`** — the gate that survives `vps`' archive. It reads newline-separated
  terms from `REDACTION_TERMS` and holds none itself, which is why it can be public. It **fails closed**
  when the variable is empty, scans the tree with fixed-string matching, and `--history` walks every commit
  with `git grep` so it can name the commit that carried a term. Proved in a scratch repository: with the
  term removed from the tree the tree scan passes and the history scan still catches the commit that had it.
  Validated in CI-shaped form with a **planted token**: staging a synthetic secret fails the hook.
- **Where the terms live now:** the Actions secret `REDACTION_TERMS` on `upayanmazumder/cluster` (17 terms,
  set with `gh secret set` from a tmpfs file — never printed, never in git) and, per the plan, the password
  manager. The CI job `redaction` runs it over **tree + history**; it is guarded to the published repository
  because this repository's own tree is *allowed* to contain the terms (`docs/plans/` documents the policy),
  and a gate that reddens the private repo gets switched off.
- **Also here:** `inventory/ports.yaml` rewritten — the collaborator names became roles, and the Postgres
  entries now describe **15432/15433 as the target**, mark **5432/5433 closed**, and mark the deleted
  NodePort duplicates `removed`.
- **Known consequence, expected:** `cluster`'s new `redaction` job **fails today** — run 36581730187 reports
  a term in commit `1fbf332`, which is pre-reseed history. The gate is working; P5-01 replaces that history
  with a single clean commit, and the job must be green before P6-04.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert. The Actions secret would be deleted separately if the job is removed.

### 23:20 IST (2026-09-29) — P4: a fresh host is reproducible (P4-02…P4-07)

- **Actor:** agent (omp session; implementation delegated to a sub-agent, reviewed and verified by the session).

  `docs/{disaster-recovery,backups,secrets}.md`. This entry.

  - **P4-02** `ansible/roles/k3s`: on a host with **no** `/etc/rancher/k3s/config.yaml`, the file is rendered
    **directly and before the installer** — the installer's first start reads it, so a `.pending` file
    rendered afterwards would be a rebuild that boots with the wrong settings. The `.pending` + diff +
    `k3s_allow_restart` path remains for an **existing** file, and a diff exit code of 2 now counts as
    "differs" rather than as an error.
  - **P4-03** the two node addresses are **derived**: `k3s_node_ip` from `ansible_default_ipv4.address`,
    `k3s_node_external_ip` from `tailscale ip -4` (asserted non-empty), and the `data_volume` device from the
    Terraform output name (`-e vps_data_linux_device=`). The **live mapping is unchanged** — node-ip is the
    public IP, node-external-ip the tailnet IP — so this is not a SAN change and needs no k3s restart.
    Verified: the live `--check` render is **byte-identical** to the node's config.yaml
    (`sha256 3fb6ec46…` on both `config.yaml` and `config.yaml.pending`, `diff` rc=0).
  - **P4-04** new `k8s/bootstrap/bootstrap.sh`: argocd namespace → `sops-age` from the offline age-cluster key
    → `sops -d …/secret/secrets.sops.yaml | kubectl apply -f -` → `helm install argocd argo/argo-cd
    --version 7.9.1 -n argocd -f k8s/platform/argocd/values.yaml` → `rollout status` → `kubectl apply -f
    k8s/bootstrap/root-app.yaml`. It **fails closed** on a missing key or a missing tool before applying
    anything, and hard-codes no secret. `shellcheck -S warning` clean. The README states plainly why a bare
    `root-app.yaml` apply is not enough (private repo, ksops age key, the Helm release) and that the

    → move the two primary IPs and the `vps_data` volume → Ansible `-e k3s_install_missing=true` → etcd
    restore **or** `bootstrap.sh` → data from R2. `recover-k3s.md` states that the node **must be named
    `vps`** (every PV carries `nodeAffinity: vps`; `vps-rebuild` is only the drill name) and restores etcd
    from R2 with the local repository as fallback. New `docs/runbooks/vcap-postgres-access.md` records the

    `backups.md` and `secrets.md`.
  - **P4-06** `admin_user` is gated behind `admin_user_enabled` (default **false**) — it was the one role
    that made a full `--check` fail, because it authorizes an SSH key for a user that does not exist in

    `host_firewall` is deliberately **not** added: the role does not exist (P7-N6 creates it) and a tag may

- **Verification:** full `--check --diff` → **`failed=0 unreachable=0`** (three runs, including a
  `--tags k3s` scoped one); `check-ansible-templates.py` → 21 templates; `check-shellcheck.sh` → OK;
  `--list-tags` shows the six tags. **`ansible-lint` could not run — it is not installed here**;

- **One measured drift, and one hypothesis disproved rather than assumed.** The `--check` run reported
  `changed=3`, one of which was `/srv/data/meghmitra-data` at mode **0755** where the role declares **0750**.
  The plausible explanation — the kubelet applying the pod's `fsGroup` to a `hostPath` volume — was **tested
  and is false**: a throwaway pod with `fsGroup: 10001` mounting a `hostPath` directory left its mode and
  ownership (`0:0 750`) untouched. The mode was converged back to `0750` with a plain `chmod` (no restart, no
  outage). The remaining candidate — the `seed-data` initContainer's `cp -a` — is untested, so the cause is
  recorded as **UNVERIFIED**; if the drift returns, the fix belongs in the tenant's Deployment.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert. These are host-configuration and documentation changes; nothing was applied to the
  node except the `chmod` above, and no k3s restart was needed or performed.

### 22:20 IST (2026-09-29) — P3-02/P3-03: nothing a GitOps change can do may cascade-delete a workload or a volume

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commits (`cluster`-independent: `vps`, mirrored) + two
  permitted break-glass operations. This entry.
- **P3-02, the four mechanisms, and the one that needed measuring:**
  1. `argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options: Delete=false,Prune=false` on all **23** manifests under
     `k8s/argocd/applications/**`, `applicationsets/apps.yaml` and `projects/*.yaml`. Root can no longer
     prune an Application, ApplicationSet or AppProject because a file moved.
  2. `preserveResourcesOnDeletion: true` on the ApplicationSet, and
     `applicationsetcontroller.policy: create-update` in `k8s/platform/argocd/values.yaml` — the
     default policy (`sync`) deletes an Application the moment its list element disappears, which is
     the cascade this closes.
  3. The `resources-finalizer.argocd.argoproj.io` removed from `root-app.yaml`, `status-page` and the
     three VCAP Applications. Root's was removed **live first** with the permitted
     `kubectl apply -f k8s/bootstrap/root-app.yaml`, then committed — the order the plan asks for, and
     the only break-glass write in this step.
  4. `Delete=false,Prune=false` on the `namespace-baseline` kustomize component and
     `k8s/monitoring/namespace.yaml`, which reaches every namespace those render.
     **Verified in the render, not assumed:** `render-all.sh` emits **17 Namespaces, all 17 carrying
     the annotation**.
- **The measurement that changed the code.** After the first sync, the ten Applications generated by
  the ApplicationSet were still unannotated: `bandit`, `bitvault`, `cas-api`, `dj-html-nodejs-web`,
  `kodesphere`, `learning-docker`, `learning-qwik`, `learning-react`, `smart-home-system-api` and the
  rest. **The set's own `metadata.annotations` does not propagate to the Applications it generates** —
  the annotation has to be on `spec.template.metadata.annotations`. Added there, committed, re-synced;
  the second pass shows **no Application without it except `root`**, which the plan's own verify
  deliberately excludes (root's object is applied by hand from `k8s/bootstrap/root-app.yaml`, which no
  Application sources, so it is not something root can prune).
  Final state: `kubectl -n argocd get app -o json | jq '… select((.metadata.finalizers//[])|length>0)'`
  → **empty**; `preserveResourcesOnDeletion` → **true**; `applicationsetcontroller.policy` →
  **create-update**; every namespace tracked by GitOps carries `Delete=false`.
- **P3-03, PVC protection, and one honest gap.** `Delete=false,Prune=false` added to
  `bitvault-postgres-pvc`, `bitvault-minio-pvc`, `grafana-pvc`, `loki-pvc`, `prometheus-pvc` — five
  files, verified by rendering every kustomization: the only PVC rendered without the annotation is
  `smart-home-system-api/smart-home-system-pvc`, which the plan names as the expected exception
  (class "none" — its PV points at a Hetzner volume deleted during S8).
  **`rankstack-mongo-pvc` cannot be fixed in git today, and this is what was found rather than
  assumed.** The plan flagged it `UNVERIFIED`; the answer is that it is rendered by
  `upayanmazumder/rankstack`'s Helm chart, and that chart's `templates/mongo.yaml` builds the PVC with
  no `metadata.annotations` and exposes no values key for one — so neither a values change nor a
  kustomize patch can reach it, because ArgoCD applies each source's output separately and a patch
  cannot cross sources. What was done instead, in order of what it actually protects:
  - the live PVC was annotated by hand (`kubectl -n rankstack annotate pvc rankstack-mongo-pvc
    argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options=Delete=false,Prune=false`), which is metadata-only and blocks the
    prune;
  - the underlying truth, checked rather than assumed: its PV
    (`pvc-fb2fad5e-…`) is `persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain`, so even an unannotated prune would
    leave the data on the node for `R-6`'s rebind procedure rather than deleting it;
  - the annotation belongs in the chart, which is an **external ask** — recorded with P2-06's asks
    rather than silently accepted. Until it lands, a fresh host rebuilt purely from git would have
    this one PVC unprotected, which is exactly the kind of gap a rebuild is supposed to surface.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** revert. All four changes are metadata; nothing is deleted by removing them.

### 21:45 IST (2026-09-29) — cluster CI red: a Terraform variable validation that 1.15 accepts and the pinned 1.7 rejects

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commits in `cluster`, this entry.
- **The failure:** `cluster` CI run `36578697979` failed in its `terraform` job at **`terraform init`**
  (*not* at `validate`, which never got to run):
  ```
  Error: Invalid reference in variable validation
    on rebuild-server.tf line 22, in variable "enable_rebuild_server":
      condition = !var.enable_rebuild_server || length(var.rebuild_server_tailscale_auth_key) > 0
  The condition for variable "enable_rebuild_server" can only refer to the variable itself.
  ```
  Every other job in that run passed (`lint`, `secret-scan`, `secret-scan-history`,
  `kustomize-and-schema`, `ansible`).
- **Why it shipped green locally, which is the part worth keeping:** the workstation has **Terraform
  1.15.9**, and 1.15 accepts a validation that refers to another variable. CI pins **`~> 1.7`**
  (1.7.5), which enforces the documented restriction. So `terraform validate` and both `plan` runs —
  including the `1 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy` one that proves the rebuild path — all passed on
  a configuration CI could not even initialise. **A local `validate` is only evidence for the version
  that ran it**; the pinned version is the one that has to accept it.
- **The fix, and why a precondition rather than a stricter validation:** the check is inherently about
  *two* variables, so no `validation` block can express it. `lifecycle { precondition { … } }` on
  `hcloud_server.rebuild` can, is evaluated exactly when the resource is planned (i.e. only when the
  flag is on), and works on 1.7.
  Verified three ways, on the real state:
  - flag on, **no** key → `Error: Resource precondition failed — enable_rebuild_server = true requires
    rebuild_server_tailscale_auth_key` (instead of a paid node whose cloud-init joins no tailnet);
  - flag on, dummy key → `Plan: 1 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`, the change being
    `hcloud_firewall_attachment.vps`;
  - flag off → no changes, `-detailed-exitcode` exits **0**.
  And compatibility with the pinned version was tested rather than assumed: Terraform **1.7.5**
  (downloaded, SHA-256 checked against HashiCorp's published sums) now runs
  `init -backend=false` → *successfully initialized* and `validate` → *Success!* over these files.
- **Also mirrored in this commit:** the Adhigrahan Radar registration and the `meghmitra` Application
  (`d0187c7`), and the `export-public.sh` change that stopped withholding that Application — see the
  P3-M4 entries.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 20:50 IST (2026-09-29) — P3-M2: the cluster-side registration for Adhigrahan Radar (additive only)

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit (`k8s/platform/storage/persistentvolumes.yaml`,
  new `k8s/argocd/projects/meghmitra.yaml`, `ansible/roles/data_volume/{defaults,tasks}`) → PRODUCTION on
  sync (additive), plus one logged host command. This entry.
- **The boundary, stated once more because it is the whole point of OD-4:** `adhigrahan-radar` owns the
  application — Deployments, Services, Ingress, NetworkPolicies, the PVC, the images and the CI. `cluster`
  owns exactly four objects for it: the **Namespace** `meghmitra`, the **static PV**, the **AppProject**
  and the **Application**. A third-party repository whose `main` this owner cannot protect must not be able
  to create cluster-scoped objects — that is ESC-001's lesson, applied before it can bite here.
- **The PV is the tenant's own specification, reproduced.** `deploy/README.md` in `adhigrahan-radar`
  says the cluster side creates it and specifies every field; `meghmitra-data-pvc-vps` is that
  specification: 1Gi, RWO, `Retain`, `storageClassName: ""`, `hostPath /srv/data/meghmitra-data`
  `type: Directory`, node affinity `vps`, `Delete=false,Prune=false`. The claim binds statically and
  therefore never to a `local-path` volume that could silently land on the node's root disk.
- **The host directory, which the PV cannot create for itself.** `type: Directory` refuses to mount a
  path that is absent, and a `hostPath` volume inherits the host's ownership — the kubelet does not
  chown it. So `/srv/data/meghmitra-data` was created as `10001:10001`, mode `0750` (the UID/GID the
  API image runs as, and it writes on every request: an unwritable store is a 500, not a log line), and
  **declared in Ansible** (`data_volume_tenant_dirs`) so a fresh host gets it too. Verified idempotent:
  the role run in `--check --diff` reports `changed=0` for that task with the directory already correct.
  VCAP's data directories are deliberately **not** in that list — they are owned by a different UID and
  this role does not own them.
- **The AppProject is as tight as the VCAP one.** `clusterResourceWhitelist: []`; namespaced kinds
  exactly `Deployment`, `Service`, `PersistentVolumeClaim`, `Ingress`, `NetworkPolicy` — what
  `kustomize build deploy/k8s` renders (9 objects, `kubeconform -strict`: 9 valid, 0 invalid) — and
  **no `Secret`**, because both GHCR packages are public so no pull Secret exists, and leaving the kind
  out means the tenant cannot put a credential into this namespace through GitOps at all.
- **Namespace added later, on purpose:** `meghmitra` joins
  `k8s/platform/storage/namespaces-tenants.yaml` in **P3-M4's** commit, not this one, so two
  Applications never render the same Namespace at once.
- **Verification so far:** `render-all.sh` → 24/24 directories; `check-app-paths.py` OK; the project and
  the PV parse and render as intended. The live checks (`PV Available`, `appproject exists`, host
  ownership) are recorded after the sync.
- **Cost impact:** none — 1Gi already reserved inside the existing 20 GB `vps-data` volume.
- **Rollback:** revert. An `Available` PV and an unused AppProject harm nothing.

### 20:35 IST (2026-09-29) — P3-07: the state-only apply, and the state-snapshot guard that failed its own first test

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** PRODUCTION CHANGE (Terraform, state-only) — plus two commits
  in `cluster`, and this entry. **Confirm: OD-5/plan P3-07.**
- **The apply changed no infrastructure.** `scripts/tf.sh plan -out=/dev/shm/tfplan` reported **0
  resource changes** and three output-only changes (`rebuild_server_hint`, `rebuild_server_ipv4`,
  `state_owner`); `scripts/tf.sh apply /dev/shm/tfplan` saved them; the plan file was `shred -u`d
  afterwards because a saved plan carries resolved values. **No resource was created, changed or
  destroyed** — this was the first apply from `cluster`, and its whole purpose was to prove that the
  state key, the resource addresses and the new owner agree.
- **Verified after the apply:**
  - `scripts/tf.sh output -raw state_owner` → `github.com/upayanmazumder/cluster//terraform`
    (the sentinel landed in state, so the state now answers "who owns me?");
  - `scripts/tf.sh plan -detailed-exitcode` → exit **0** (no drift);
  - a state snapshot exists in `~/.local/share/vps-tfstate-backup/` (this is the P3-07 requirement
    that is easy to fake and therefore worth naming exactly — see below).
- **The guard failed its first test, and that is the entry's real content.** `scripts/tf.sh` snapshots
  state before `apply`/`destroy`/`import`/`state`. Its first version called
  `sops exec-env "$secrets" terraform state pull`, and **`sops exec-env` takes a single command
  *string*, not a command plus argv** — it runs it through a shell. Passing them separately makes sops
  read `terraform` as the file to decrypt and fail with `missing file to decrypt`. The script's own
  error path then printed a warning and continued, so **the first apply ran with no snapshot** and the
  only sign was a line that `tail` had cut off.
  Two lessons, both cheap to carry forward:
  1. `sops exec-env FILE 'command string'` — one string, always;
  2. **a warning on the guard's own failure path is a silent guard.** The snapshot step was then
     re-tested directly rather than assumed: `scripts/tf.sh state list` created
     `~/.local/share/vps-tfstate-backup/20260929T135420Z-serial2.tfstate`, so the guard now
     demonstrably works before the operations that need it.
  The apply itself was safe to have run without a snapshot — it changed no resource — which is luck,
  not design, and the reason the fix ships in the same commit as the report of it.
- **Also in this step:** `vps/scripts/private/mirror-to-cluster.sh`, the mirror rule expressed as code.
  Since P3-04/P3-06 the two trees are **deliberately** different, so a whole-tree copy now reverts the
  cluster's own content; the script mirrors the export while excluding exactly the paths that differ by
  design (`terraform/README.md`, `.github/workflows/validate.yml`, and the `cluster`-only Terraform
  files it never deletes), refuses if the export's redaction gate fails, and supports `--check`.
- **Cost impact:** none.
- **Rollback:** `sops exec-env "$TF_SECRETS" 'terraform state push -force
  ~/.local/share/vps-tfstate-backup/<snapshot>'` from `cluster/terraform` — outputs only, so this is
  cosmetic. No infrastructure change to undo.

### 20:20 IST (2026-09-29) — P3-06: `vps` holds no Terraform configuration; `cluster` is the only owner

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit — `terraform/*.tf` removed (16 files),
  `terraform/.terraform.lock.hcl`, `terraform/.tflint.hcl`, `terraform/secrets.enc.env` removed,
  `terraform/README.md` replaced with a pointer, the `terraform` CI job removed from
  `.github/workflows/validate.yml`, and two `terraform/storage.tf` references in
  `ansible/roles/data_volume/tasks/main.yml` retargeted at the `cluster` repository. This entry.
- **Why removal rather than a second copy kept in step:** two configurations against one state key
  (`s3://vps-tfstate/vps/terraform.tfstate`) make a plan run from the wrong checkout
  indistinguishable from a correct one until it applies. That is the failure this phase exists to
  close, and a "kept in sync by discipline" copy is the same failure with extra steps.
- **What did NOT happen, deliberately:** no `init -migrate-state`, no `state mv`, no `state rm`, no
  apply. The state key is unchanged (OD-5), the resource addresses are identical, and the only
  Terraform action taken was a `plan` — twice, both read-only
  (`-lock=false`), the second proving the rebuild path still offers exactly
  `1 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`.
- **The `vps` CI job is gone; `cluster`'s is not.** This is the one place the two
  `validate.yml` files are now deliberately different. `cluster` keeps `terraform fmt` /
  `init -backend=false` / `validate` / `tflint` over the files it owns; `vps` has no configuration to
  lint, and a job whose `working-directory` does not exist fails for a reason nobody should have to
  read twice. Consequence to remember during the mirroring steps of P6: **do not run the export over
  `.github/workflows/validate.yml`** — the export is sourced from this repository, and copying its
  version into `cluster` would delete the Terraform job there.
- **`.tflint.hcl` went with the `.tf` files**: a linter configuration for a directory with no
  Terraform in it is not a spare part, it is a claim that someone should run it.
- **Verification:** `git ls-files 'terraform/*.tf' 'terraform/secrets.enc.env' | wc -l` → **0**;
  `test ! -d terraform/.terraform` → true; `terraform/README.md` names the new owner, the state key,
  `scripts/tf.sh` and the credential path; `ansible/roles/data_volume/tasks/main.yml` still parses as
  YAML; the only remaining in-repo references to `terraform/` are the pointer itself and the export
  script's exclusion rule.
- **Rollback:** `git revert` — but note that a revert would restore the second owner, so it should
  only be used together with reverting the `cluster` commit that took ownership. State is untouched
  either way.
- **Cost impact:** none.

### 20:05 IST (2026-09-29) — P3-01/P3-04/P3-05/P5-05: `cluster` becomes the sole Terraform owner, and `secrets.enc.env` leaves both repositories

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commits in `cluster` (`terraform/rebuild-server.tf`,
  `terraform/firewall.tf`, `terraform/outputs.tf`, `terraform/cloudflare.tf`, `.gitignore`, new
  `scripts/tf.sh`, `git rm terraform/secrets.enc.env`), in `vps`
  (`scripts/private/export-public.sh`), this entry. **No apply.**
- **P3-01 gate first, and it passed:** the Applications `cluster` defines against the Applications that
  exist, `meghmitra` aside, differ by **`meghmitra` only** (live-only) — the single expected line.
- **P3-04 — `rebuild-server.tf` ported, with the firewall ownership bug fixed.** The file moved into
  `cluster/terraform/` and three things changed:
  - **`firewall_ids` removed from `hcloud_server.rebuild`.** `hcloud_firewall_attachment.vps` now owns
    membership for both machines (`server_ids = concat([hcloud_server.vps.id],
    hcloud_server.rebuild[*].id)`). Two owners for one attribute means whichever refreshes last wins,
    and the losing outcome — a rebuild node with no rules, or the *existing* node detached by a plan
    that only meant to create the rebuild server — is silent until it is an outage.
  - **a `validation` on the variable**: `enable_rebuild_server = true` with an empty auth key would
    otherwise produce a paid node whose cloud-init joins no tailnet, i.e. unreachable and only
    discoverable afterwards.
  - **`lifecycle { ignore_changes = [user_data] }`**, because `user_data` embeds the auth key: without
    it a later plan would want to rewrite a sensitive field whose diff cannot be read, and the change
    would be approved blind. The comment that pairs with it says the consequence out loud — never
    `-out` a plan while a real key is supplied, since the plan file would carry it.
  - `.gitignore` gained `*.plan`, `tfplan`, `plan.out` for the same reason.
- **P3-05 — one state, one owner, and a guard that survives a mistake.** `outputs.tf` gains a
  `state_owner` sentinel (`github.com/upayanmazumder/cluster//terraform`) so the state itself answers
  "which configuration owns this?", which a directory listing cannot. `scripts/tf.sh` refuses to run
  unless the checkout's `origin` is `upayanmazumder/cluster`, reads the provider credentials from
  `${TF_SECRETS:-~/.config/vps/terraform/secrets.enc.env}`, and **automatically snapshots state to
  `~/.local/share/vps-tfstate-backup/<UTC>-serial<N>.tfstate` (dir 700, file 600) before any
  `apply`/`destroy`/`import`/`state`**. `prevent_destroy` now covers both R2 buckets — the backup leg is
  the only copy of the cluster that is not on the cluster, and R2 keeps no versioning to recover with.
- **P5-05 — `terraform/secrets.enc.env` is out of both repositories (OD-10).** It now lives at
  `~/.config/vps/terraform/secrets.enc.env` (mode 600, directory 700; verified it still decrypts to
  **4 keys**), copied to the password manager and the offline medium per P4-01. `export-public.sh`
  excludes it by name with the reasoning inline; `cluster` no longer tracks it. Nothing in CI used
  it — CI runs `terraform init -backend=false` plus `validate`, which needs no provider credentials —
  so the only cost is that a fresh machine must restore it before planning. The Cloudflare token in it
  has **no expiry**, which is exactly why encrypted-and-published was rejected (OD-9 declines rotation
  at this stage, so published ciphertext would be permanently harvestable for no benefit).
- **Verification (all run, all observed):**
  - `terraform fmt -check` clean; `terraform validate` → `Success! The configuration is valid.`;
  - `scripts/tf.sh` → `shellcheck -S warning` clean, and `scripts/check-shellcheck.sh` → `OK`
    (6 scripts + 6 templates);
  - `terraform plan` (rebuild off) → **0 resource changes**, 3 output-only changes;
  - `terraform plan -refresh=false -var enable_rebuild_server=true -var
    rebuild_server_tailscale_auth_key=tskey-dummy` → **`1 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`**, and the
    change is `hcloud_firewall_attachment.vps` — nothing else;
  - `terraform init` against the R2 backend succeeded, reusing the lock file's provider versions
    (`cloudflare 4.52.9`, `hcloud 1.69.0`).
- **Not done here, deliberately:** `vps/terraform` still exists (P3-06) and no apply has run (P3-07).
  Nothing between P3-04 and P3-06 applies anything.
- **Cost impact:** none — the plan changes create nothing.
- **Rollback:** revert the `cluster` commit; `terraform state push -force <snapshot>` if a state write
  had gone wrong (none did).

### 19:45 IST (2026-09-29) — P2-01/P2-05 follow-up: the project is locked down, the namespaces are adopted — and `CreateNamespace=true` was proven to need the `Namespace` kind

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit (the two remaining
  `k8s/argocd/applications/vcap/*.yaml`), this entry. **Corrects the entry below**, which claimed the
  change touched "the three `k8s/argocd/applications/vcap/*.yaml`".
- **What actually happened, and it is the useful part.** The earlier edit used a single
  `replace_all` pass over **one** file, so `vcap-backend-dev.yaml` lost `CreateNamespace=true` and
  `managedNamespaceMetadata` while `vcap-backend-staging.yaml` and `vcap-frontend-staging.yaml` kept them.
  Root then applied the new, empty `clusterResourceWhitelist` alongside them, and the two stale specs failed
  visibly:
  ```
  Namespace  vcap-staging  SyncFailed  resource :Namespace is not permitted in project vcap
  ```
- **That failure answers the plan's `UNVERIFIED` item.** On ArgoCD **v2.14.11**, `CreateNamespace=true`
  (and `managedNamespaceMetadata`, which implies it) makes the destination `Namespace` a resource of the sync,
  and it **does** require `Namespace` in the project's `clusterResourceWhitelist`. The plan assumed it might
  not matter because P2-05 removes the option in the same commit; ordering made it matter, and the cluster
  said so rather than the plan's guess. Recorded here so the next person does not have to re-derive it:
  **if a project does not permit `Namespace`, no Application in it may set `CreateNamespace=true`.**
- **The fix:** both files now carry the same comment and the same
  `syncOptions: [ServerSideApply=true, ApplyOutOfSyncOnly=true]` as `vcap-backend-dev.yaml`.
- **Verified before this entry was written:** `AppProject vcap` →
  `clusterResourceWhitelist: []`, `namespaceResourceWhitelist` = the 15 intended kinds;
  `Namespace vcap-dev` and `vcap-staging` → `argocd.argoproj.io/instance: storage`,
  `pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: baseline`; `IngressRouteTCP vcap-dev/vcap-postgres-dev` →
  `instance: vcap-backend-dev`, `Prune=false`. `storage` is `Synced/Healthy` at `dd2dd8b`.
- **Rollback:** revert. `Prune=false`/`Delete=false` on the namespaces and the edge means nothing is deleted.

### 19:35 IST (2026-09-29) — P2-01/P2-02/P2-05/P1-04: the `vcap` project can no longer escalate, and the tenant namespaces and Postgres edge have a cluster owner

- **Actor:** agent (omp session). **Type:** git commit (`k8s/argocd/projects/vcap.yaml`,
  `k8s/platform/storage/{namespaces-tenants.yaml,kustomization.yaml}`, the three
  `k8s/argocd/applications/vcap/*.yaml`, new `k8s/apps/vcap/edge/{dev,staging}/`), this entry.
- **P2-01 — the escalation, closed at the project layer.** The `vcap` AppProject granted, at cluster scope,
  `Namespace`, `ClusterRole`, `ClusterRoleBinding` and **all of `keda.sh/*`**, and every namespaced kind via
  `group: "*" / kind: "*"`. With tenant `main` unprotected, a push to a VCAP chart could therefore create a
  ClusterRoleBinding to `cluster-admin` (ESC-001). `clusterResourceWhitelist` is now **empty** and
  `namespaceResourceWhitelist` lists exactly the 15 kinds the three Applications actually render — read off
  each app's live `status.resources` on 2026-09-29 — plus `traefik.io/IngressRouteTCP` for the edge below.
  `ClusterTriggerAuthentication`, `Role`, `RoleBinding`, `ResourceQuota`, `LimitRange`, `Pod` and the other
  `traefik.io` routing kinds stay denied.
- **P2-05/P2-02 — the namespaces have an owner and a Pod Security level.** `vcap-dev` and `vcap-staging` were
  created as a side effect of `CreateNamespace=true` and owned by no manifest; they are now declared in
  `k8s/platform/storage/namespaces-tenants.yaml`, applied by the `storage` Application (project `platform`,
  which permits cluster-scoped `Namespace`), with `Delete=false,Prune=false` and Pod Security
  `enforce: baseline` + `warn: restricted`. The three Applications lost `CreateNamespace=true` and
  `managedNamespaceMetadata` **in the same commit**: two owners for one object is how a namespace picks up a
  field nobody meant to set. `baseline` was measured safe by a server-side dry-run on 2026-09-28 (no
  violation for any VCAP workload); the "S6 plans postgres.hostPort" comment that argued against `enforce`
  was obsolete — no VCAP chart template renders `hostPort`.
- **P1-04 — the public Postgres edge is in git.** `IngressRouteTCP vcap-postgres-{dev,staging}` existed only
  in the cluster (created 2026-07-26 by hand), so a rebuild from git could not reproduce it and nothing
  reviewed a change to it. Both are now `k8s/apps/vcap/edge/<env>/`, exported from the live objects with
  behaviour unchanged (same entrypoint, same `HostSNI("*")` route, same service) and `Prune=false`, wired in
  as a fourth source on the two backend Applications. Port and TLS are **not** changed here — that is P7-N7,
  gated on P2-08.
- **What this deliberately does not do:** revert the tenant's own same-namespace NetworkPolicy that broke the
  public path (correct for everything except the deliberate edge), and reopen 5432/5433. Measured on both
  instances the same hour: `ssl = off`, the only login role is **superuser** (`vcap_user`, `rolsuper=t`,
  `rolcreatedb=t`, `rolcreaterole=t`), and `pg_hba.conf` ends `host all all all scram-sha-256`. Reopening now
  would publish a TLS-less superuser database.
- **Verification:** `kustomize build` on both edge dirs and on `k8s/platform/storage` renders the expected
  objects; `scripts/render-all.sh` → 24/24 directories (was 22/22); `scripts/check-app-paths.py` OK;
  `scripts/check-secrets.sh` OK. The live proofs (`auth can-i` loop, the escalation canary, PSA probe) are in
  the next entry.
- **Rollback:** revert the commit. `Prune=false` on the namespaces and the IngressRouteTCPs means the objects
  stay; the project reverts to its permissive whitelists.
- **Cost impact:** none.
